$T_{\theta_0}M$, and the canonical choice is the parallel transport of
$(M,g)$ along the minimizing geodesic joining the two points, which we
compute first.

\subsection*{The minimizing geodesics of $M$ and their parallel transport}

The metric $g$ was constructed in Section~\ref{sec:basic_setup} so that
$\pi$ is a Riemannian submersion from the \textit{flat} manifold
$\Theta \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{pq+1}$, and
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} exploited this to make the
exponential map affine in the horizontal lift.
The same structure turns the parallel transport into an explicit linear
ordinary differential equation.

Parallel transport is an operation attached to a \textit{curve}, whereas
the object we need --- the map written $\Pi_{x_1}^{x_2}$ in Section II of
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} --- is attached to a \textit{pair of points}.
The two are reconciled in the way that paper prescribes: between two
points close enough to each other, transport is taken along the unique
distance minimizing geodesic joining them.
The distance in question is the Riemannian distance of $(M,g)$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:d_M_def}
  d_M(x,y) := \inf\curly{\int_a^b \abs{\dot{c}(\tau)}\,\mathrm{d}\tau
  \ : \ c : [a,b] \to M \text{ piecewise smooth},\ c(a) = x,\ c(b) = y},
\end{equation}
the infimum of the $g$-lengths of the curves joining $x$ to $y$
(\citealp{Lee_2018}, Chapter 2).
That this infimum is taken over a nonempty set, so that $d_M$ is finite
on all of $M \times M$, is a global property of $M$; it is proved in
Lemma~\ref{thm:M_connected} and is needed only there.
Nothing in the present section uses it, because the joining curve will be
exhibited and the infimum computed by hand, and what makes that possible
is that Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} has already produced the
geodesic balls of $M$.
Indeed, that proposition makes $\text{Exp}_{[\bm{A},s]}$ a diffeomorphism
of the ball $\mathcal{E}_{[\bm{A},s]}$ of radius
$\min\bracket{\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}),s}$ in $T_{[\bm{A},s]}M$ onto the open
set $U_{[\bm{A},s]} \subseteq M$, which is precisely what it means for
$U_{[\bm{A},s]}$ to be a \textit{geodesic ball} centred at $[\bm{A},s]$
(\citealp{Lee_2018}, Chapter 6), and \eqref{eq:logarithmic_def} writes in
closed form the radial geodesic reaching any prescribed point of it.
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} assembles the two ingredients
into a single statement: the minimizing geodesic between two points, and
the transport along it.

Two preliminaries are needed.
The first makes the orthogonal splitting
$T_{(\bm{A},s)}\Theta = V_{(\bm{A},s)} \oplus H_{(\bm{A},s)}$ of
Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical} explicit, through a Lyapunov
equation that will turn out to govern the transport as well; it also shows
that the horizontal lift \eqref{eq:lift_def} depends smoothly on its base
point, which is used repeatedly below.
The second is the submersion identity, in the form we need.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:lyapunov_lemma}
Let $\bm{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ have rank $q$ and put
$\bm{P}_{\bm{A}} := \bm{A}^T\bm{A}$, a positive definite $q \times q$
matrix.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For every $\bm{K} \in \mathbb{R}^{q \times q}$ the Lyapunov
  equation

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:lyapunov}
    \bm{P}_{\bm{A}}\bm{\Omega} + \bm{\Omega}\bm{P}_{\bm{A}} = \bm{K}
  \end{equation}
  has a unique solution $\bm{\Omega} =: \mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}}(\bm{K})$.
  The map $\mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}}$ is linear, the map
  $(\bm{A},\bm{K}) \mapsto \mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}}(\bm{K})$ is smooth, and
  $\mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}}(\bm{K})$ is skew-symmetric whenever $\bm{K}$ is.
  \item For every $(\bm{V},c) \in T_{(\bm{A},s)}\Theta$ the orthogonal
  projection $\mathcal{H}_{(\bm{A},s)}$ onto the horizontal space is

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:horizontal_projection}
    \mathcal{H}_{(\bm{A},s)}\bracket{(\bm{V},c)}
    = \bracket{\bm{V} - \bm{A}\,\mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}}
    \bracket{\bm{A}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{A}},\ c},
  \end{equation}
  the subtracted term being the orthogonal projection of $(\bm{V},c)$
  onto $V_{(\bm{A},s)}$.
  \item Consequently
  $\iota_{(\bm{A},s)}\bracket{\mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{A},s)}((\bm{V},c))}
  = \mathcal{H}_{(\bm{A},s)}\bracket{(\bm{V},c)}$, and this expression is
  smooth in $(\bm{A},s,\bm{V},c)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.} Diagonalize
$\bm{P}_{\bm{A}} = \bm{O}\,\text{diag}(p_1,\ldots,p_q)\,\bm{O}^T$ with
$\bm{O} \in O(q)$ and every $p_i > 0$.
Conjugating \eqref{eq:lyapunov} by $\bm{O}$ turns the linear operator
$\bm{\Omega} \mapsto \bm{P}_{\bm{A}}\bm{\Omega}+\bm{\Omega}\bm{P}_{\bm{A}}$
into the entrywise multiplication by $p_i+p_j > 0$, which is invertible;
existence, uniqueness and linearity follow.
Smoothness holds because the operator depends polynomially on $\bm{A}$ and
the inverse of an invertible linear map depends smoothly on it.
If $\bm{K}^T = -\bm{K}$, transposing \eqref{eq:lyapunov} gives
$\bm{P}_{\bm{A}}\bracket{-\bm{\Omega}^T}
+ \bracket{-\bm{\Omega}^T}\bm{P}_{\bm{A}} = -\bm{K}^T = \bm{K}$,
so $-\bm{\Omega}^T$ solves the same equation and uniqueness forces
$\bm{\Omega}^T = -\bm{\Omega}$.

\textbf{2.} By Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical} the vertical
space is $\curly{(\bm{A}\bm{\Omega},0) : \bm{\Omega}^T = -\bm{\Omega}}$,
so the orthogonal projection of $(\bm{V},c)$ onto it is
$(\bm{A}\bm{\Omega}_*,0)$, where $\bm{\Omega}_*$ minimizes
$\|\bm{V}-\bm{A}\bm{\Omega}\|_F^2$ over skew-symmetric $\bm{\Omega}$; the
scalar component contributes the constant $c^2$ and plays no role.
Differentiating this strictly convex quadratic in a skew-symmetric
direction $\bm{K}$ gives the stationarity condition
$\tr\bracket{\bm{K}^T\bm{A}^T\bracket{\bm{V}-\bm{A}\bm{\Omega}_*}} = 0$
for every skew-symmetric $\bm{K}$, which says exactly that the
skew-symmetric part of
$\bm{A}^T\bm{V}-\bm{P}_{\bm{A}}\bm{\Omega}_*$ vanishes:

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{A}^T\bm{V} - \bm{V}^T\bm{A}
  = \bm{P}_{\bm{A}}\bm{\Omega}_* - \bm{\Omega}_*^T\bm{P}_{\bm{A}}
  = \bm{P}_{\bm{A}}\bm{\Omega}_* + \bm{\Omega}_*\bm{P}_{\bm{A}} .
\end{equation*}
This is \eqref{eq:lyapunov} with the skew-symmetric right hand side
$\bm{K} = \bm{A}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{A}$, and part 1 identifies
$\bm{\Omega}_* = \mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}}(\bm{K})$ and confirms that it is
skew-symmetric, as it must be; subtracting $(\bm{A}\bm{\Omega}_*,0)$ from
$(\bm{V},c)$ gives \eqref{eq:horizontal_projection}.

\textbf{3.} The vector $\mathcal{H}_{(\bm{A},s)}((\bm{V},c))$ is
horizontal and differs from $(\bm{V},c)$ by a vertical vector, so it has
the same image under $\mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{A},s)}$, whose kernel is
$V_{(\bm{A},s)}$ by \eqref{eq:vertical_space}.
Smoothness is that of \eqref{eq:horizontal_projection}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:submersion_derivative}
Let $c : I \to \Theta$ be a smooth curve with horizontal velocity at every
time, let $\gamma := \pi \circ c$, and let $\bm{x}$ be a smooth vector
field along $\gamma$, with horizontal lift
$\tilde{\bm{x}}(t) := \iota_{c(t)}\bracket{\bm{x}(t)}$.
Then the horizontal lift of the covariant derivative $D_t\bm{x}$ of
$(M,g)$ is the horizontal part of the ordinary derivative of
$\tilde{\bm{x}}$:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:submersion_derivative}
  \iota_{c(t)}\bracket{D_t\bm{x}(t)}
  = \mathcal{H}_{c(t)}\bracket{\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}}(t)} .
\end{equation}
In particular $\bm{x}$ is parallel along $\gamma$ if and only if
$\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}}(t) \in V_{c(t)}$ for every $t$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $A(\bm{x}) := D_t\bm{x}$ and
$B(\bm{x}) := \mathrm{d}\pi_{c(t)}
\bracket{\mathcal{H}_{c(t)}\bracket{\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}}}}$; both are
$\mathbb{R}$-linear operators on smooth vector fields along $\gamma$.
Both satisfy the Leibniz rule $L(f\bm{x}) = f'\bm{x}+fL(\bm{x})$ for
$f \in C^{\infty}(I)$: for $A$ this is part of the definition of the
covariant derivative along a curve, and for $B$ it follows from
$\iota_{c}(f\bm{x}) = f\tilde{\bm{x}}$, from
$\frac{d}{dt}(f\tilde{\bm{x}}) = f'\tilde{\bm{x}}+f\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}}$
and from $\mathcal{H}_{c(t)}(\tilde{\bm{x}}) = \tilde{\bm{x}}$.
They agree on extendible fields.
Indeed, let $Y$ be a smooth vector field on an open subset of $M$ and
$\bm{x} = Y \circ \gamma$; then $\tilde{\bm{x}} = \tilde{Y} \circ c$ with
$\tilde{Y}$ the basic field lifting $Y$, so $\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}}$ is the
Euclidean covariant derivative
$\tilde{\nabla}_{\dot{c}}\tilde{Y}$ of $\Theta$, whose horizontal part is
the lift of $\nabla_{\mathrm{d}\pi(\dot{c})}Y = \nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}Y
= D_t\bm{x}$ by the fundamental identity for Riemannian submersions
(\citealp{O_neill_1967}, Section 2; \citealp{Valencia_2021}, Section 3),
which applies because $\dot{c}$ is horizontal.
A covariant derivative along a curve is uniquely determined by
$\mathbb{R}$-linearity, the Leibniz rule and its values on extendible
fields (see \citealp{Lee_2018}, Chapter 4, the uniqueness clause of the
existence theorem for covariant derivatives along curves), so $A = B$,
which is \eqref{eq:submersion_derivative}.
The last sentence follows because $\iota_{c(t)}$ is injective and
$\ker \mathcal{H}_{c(t)} = V_{c(t)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The minimizing geodesic and its parallel transport]\label{thm:parallel_transport}
Let $[\bm{A},s] \in M$ and let $[\bm{B},t] \in U_{[\bm{A},s]}$.
Put

\begin{equation}\label{eq:geodesic_data}
  \bm{Q} := \bm{Q}(\bm{A},\bm{B}), \qquad
  \bm{V} := \bm{B}\bm{Q}-\bm{A}, \qquad
  c := t-s, \qquad
  \bm{A}_{\tau} := \bm{A}+\tau\bm{V},
\end{equation}
with $\bm{Q}(\, \cdot \,,\, \cdot \,)$ the orthogonal matrix
\eqref{eq:Q_A_B_def}, and let

\begin{equation}\label{eq:geodesic_gamma}
  \gamma(\tau) := \squared{\bm{A}_{\tau},\ s+\tau c} \in M,
  \qquad
  \tilde{\gamma}(\tau) := \bracket{\bm{A}_{\tau},\ s+\tau c} \in \Theta,
  \qquad \tau \in [0,1].
\end{equation}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{The curve.}
  Neither $\gamma$ nor its length depends on the representatives chosen
  for $[\bm{A},s]$ and for $[\bm{B},t]$.
  The curve $\gamma$ is the geodesic of
  Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} issued from
  $\gamma(0) = [\bm{A},s]$ with initial velocity

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:gamma_velocity}
    \bm{v} := \text{Exp}^{-1}_{[\bm{A},s]}\bracket{[\bm{B},t]}
    \in \mathcal{E}_{[\bm{A},s]},
    \qquad
    \iota_{(\bm{A},s)}(\bm{v}) = (\bm{V},c);
  \end{equation}
  it has constant speed $\abs{\bm{v}}$ and ends at
  $\gamma(1) = [\bm{B},t]$, and $\tilde{\gamma}$ is its horizontal lift
  through $(\bm{A},s)$, a straight line of constant horizontal velocity
  $(\bm{V},c)$.
  Its length realizes the Riemannian distance \eqref{eq:d_M_def}:

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:d_M_formula}
    d_M\bracket{[\bm{A},s],\ [\bm{B},t]}
    = \abs{\bm{v}}
    = \sqrt{\|\bm{B}\bm{Q}(\bm{A},\bm{B})-\bm{A}\|_F^2+(t-s)^2},
  \end{equation}
  and, if $[\bm{B},t] \neq [\bm{A},s]$, then $\gamma$ is, up to
  reparametrization, the \textit{only} curve in $M$ from $[\bm{A},s]$ to
  $[\bm{B},t]$ of that length.
  Finally $\rank(\bm{A}_{\tau}) = q$ for every $\tau \in [0,1]$, so
  Lemma~\ref{thm:lyapunov_lemma} applies to $\bm{A}_{\tau}$; we abbreviate
  $\mathcal{L}_{\tau} := \mathcal{L}_{\bm{A}_{\tau}}$.

  \item \textbf{The transport equation.}
  Let $\tau_0 \in [0,1]$ and
  $(\bm{X}_0,\beta_0) \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q} \times \mathbb{R}$.
  The linear ordinary differential equation

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:transport_ODE}
    \dot{\bm{X}}(\tau)
    = \bm{A}_{\tau}\,\bm{\Omega}(\tau),
    \qquad
    \bm{\Omega}(\tau) := \mathcal{L}_{\tau}\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau)^T\bm{V}
    - \bm{V}^T\bm{X}(\tau)},
    \qquad
    \dot{\beta}(\tau) = 0,
  \end{equation}
  has a unique solution on all of $[0,1]$ subject to
  $\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau_0),\beta(\tau_0)} = (\bm{X}_0,\beta_0)$; it is
  linear in $(\bm{X}_0,\beta_0)$ and smooth in
  $\bracket{\tau,\tau_0,\bm{A},\bm{V},\bm{X}_0,\beta_0}$.
  If moreover $(\bm{X}_0,\beta_0) \in H_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau_0)}$, then
  the solution stays horizontal, that is
  $\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau),\beta(\tau)} \in H_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau)}$ for
  every $\tau \in [0,1]$, and it preserves the Euclidean norm:
  $\|\bm{X}(\tau)\|_F^2+\beta(\tau)^2$ is constant.

  \item \textbf{The transport map.}
  For $\tau_0,\tau_1 \in [0,1]$ write

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Pi_curve}
    \Pi(\gamma)_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1} :
    T_{\gamma(\tau_0)}M \longrightarrow T_{\gamma(\tau_1)}M
  \end{equation}
  for the parallel transport of $(M,g)$ along $\gamma$ from time $\tau_0$
  to time $\tau_1$.
  It exists, it is a linear isometry, its inverse is
  $\Pi(\gamma)_{\tau_1}^{\tau_0}$, and, read through the horizontal lifts
  $\iota_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau)}$, it is the flow of
  \eqref{eq:transport_ODE}:

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Pi_flow}
    \Pi(\gamma)_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}\bracket{
    \mathrm{d}\pi_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau_0)}\bracket{(\bm{X}_0,\beta_0)}}
    = \mathrm{d}\pi_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau_1)}
    \bracket{\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau_1),\beta(\tau_1)}},
    \qquad (\bm{X}_0,\beta_0) \in H_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau_0)} .
  \end{equation}
  By part 1 the curve $\gamma$ is determined, up to reparametrization, by
  its two endpoints alone, and parallel transport is unchanged by a
  reparametrization; the notation

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Pi_def}
    \Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{B},t]} := \Pi(\gamma)_1^0 :
    T_{[\bm{B},t]}M \longrightarrow T_{[\bm{A},s]}M
  \end{equation}
  is therefore unambiguous, and this is the map $\Pi_{x_1}^{x_2}$ of
  Section II of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}.
  Explicitly, let $\bm{z} \in T_{[\bm{B},t]}M$, let

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:z_lift}
    (\bm{Y},\beta) := \iota_{(\bm{B}\bm{Q},\, t)}(\bm{z}),
    \qquad
    (\bm{B}\bm{Q},\, t) = \bracket{\bm{A}+\bm{V},\ s+c} = \tilde{\gamma}(1),
  \end{equation}
  be the horizontal lift of $\bm{z}$ at the aligned representative
  $(\bm{B}\bm{Q},t)$ of $[\bm{B},t]$, and let $\bm{X}$ be the unique
  solution on $[0,1]$ of the terminal value problem

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:transport_backward}
    \dot{\bm{X}}(\tau) = \bm{A}_{\tau}\,\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
    \bracket{\bm{X}(\tau)^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}(\tau)},
    \qquad
    \bm{X}(1) = \bm{Y} .
  \end{equation}
  Then

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Pi_formula}
    \Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{B},t]}(\bm{z})
    = \mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{A},s)}\bracket{\bracket{\bm{X}(0),\ \beta}},
    \qquad
    \abs{\Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{B},t]}(\bm{z})} = \abs{\bm{z}},
  \end{equation}
  and $\bracket{\bm{A},\bm{V},\bm{Y},\beta} \mapsto
  \bracket{\bm{X}(0),\beta}$ is smooth, and linear in $(\bm{Y},\beta)$.
  In particular $\Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{A},s]} = \text{id}$; and
  $\bm{X} \equiv \bm{V}$ solves \eqref{eq:transport_backward} for
  $\bm{Y} = \bm{V}$, so that
  $\Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{B},t]}\bracket{\dot{\gamma}(1)}
  = \dot{\gamma}(0) = \bm{v}$, as a geodesic requires.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.} Since $[\bm{B},t] \in U_{[\bm{A},s]}$, part 4 of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} gives
$\bm{v} = \mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{A},s)}\bracket{(\bm{V},c)}$ with
$(\bm{V},c)$ as in \eqref{eq:geodesic_data}, and part 1 of the same
proposition, applied to $\bm{v}$, makes
$\bm{A}^T\bracket{\bm{A}+\bm{V}} = \bm{A}^T\bm{B}\bm{Q}$ symmetric; hence
$\bm{A}^T\bm{V}$ is symmetric and $(\bm{V},c)$ is horizontal at
$(\bm{A},s)$ by part 3 of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical}.
This is \eqref{eq:gamma_velocity}, and
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} then identifies
$\gamma(\tau) = \text{Exp}_{[\bm{A},s]}(\tau\bm{v})$ as the geodesic
issued from $[\bm{A},s]$ with velocity $\bm{v}$, with
$\gamma(1) = \squared{\bm{A}+\bm{V},\ s+c} = [\bm{B}\bm{Q},t] = [\bm{B},t]$
because $\bm{Q} \in O(q)$.
Both $\text{Exp}_{[\bm{A},s]}$ and $\bm{v}$ are defined without reference
to representatives, so $\gamma$ is too.
Since $\bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{V} = \bm{A}^T\bm{V}+\tau\bm{V}^T\bm{V}$ is
symmetric for every $\tau$, the constant velocity $(\bm{V},c)$ of the
straight line $\tilde{\gamma}$ is horizontal at every time, and
$\pi \circ \tilde{\gamma} = \gamma$; so $\tilde{\gamma}$ is the horizontal
lift of $\gamma$ through $(\bm{A},s)$ and, $\pi$ being a Riemannian
submersion, $\abs{\dot{\gamma}(\tau)} = \abs{\bm{v}}$ for every $\tau$ and
$\gamma$ has $g$-length $\abs{\bm{v}}$.

By part 2 of Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} the map
$\text{Exp}_{[\bm{A},s]}$ is a diffeomorphism of the ball
$\mathcal{E}_{[\bm{A},s]}$ onto the open set $U_{[\bm{A},s]}$, so
$U_{[\bm{A},s]}$ is a geodesic ball centred at $[\bm{A},s]$ and $\gamma$
is the radial geodesic of that ball reaching $[\bm{B},t]$.
Proposition 6.11 of \cite{Lee_2018} therefore says that, for
$[\bm{B},t] \neq [\bm{A},s]$, the curve $\gamma$ is, up to
reparametrization, the unique curve of least length in $M$ joining the two
points.
In particular every competitor in \eqref{eq:d_M_def} is at least as long
as $\gamma$, so the infimum is attained at $\gamma$ and equals
$\abs{\bm{v}} = \sqrt{\|\bm{V}\|_F^2+c^2}$, the horizontal lift
$\iota_{(\bm{A},s)}$ being a linear isometry for the Euclidean metric
\eqref{eq:euclidean_metric}; this is \eqref{eq:d_M_formula}, and no global
property of $M$ was used to obtain it.
When $[\bm{B},t] = [\bm{A},s]$ one has $\bm{Q} = \bm{I}_q$,
$(\bm{V},c) = (\bm{0},0)$ and $\gamma$ constant, and
\eqref{eq:d_M_formula} holds trivially.

Finally $\|\bm{V}\| \leqslant \|\bm{V}\|_F \leqslant \abs{\bm{v}}
< \min\bracket{\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}),s}$, so Weyl's perturbation
inequality, as quoted in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}, gives
$\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}_{\tau}) \geqslant \sigma_{\min}(\bm{A})
- \tau\|\bm{V}\| > 0$ for $\tau \in [0,1]$; hence $\bm{A}_{\tau}$ has
rank $q$.

\textbf{2.} By part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:lyapunov_lemma} the right hand
side of \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} is linear in $(\bm{X},\beta)$, with
coefficients that are smooth in $\tau$ and in $(\bm{A},\bm{V})$ on the
open set where $\bm{A}_{\tau}$ has rank $q$, a set which by part 1
contains $[0,1]$.
Linear systems have solutions that exist and are unique on the whole
interval, whatever the time $\tau_0$ at which the data are prescribed, and
that depend smoothly on the time and linearly on the data
(\citealp{Lee_2018}, Theorem 4.31); adjoining $(\bm{A},\bm{V})$ to the
unknown as extra components with vanishing derivative turns the
dependence on those parameters into a dependence on initial conditions,
which is smooth as well (\citealp{Lee_2013}, Theorem D.1 and Theorem D.6).
By the same lemma each $\bm{\Omega}(\tau)$ is skew-symmetric, because
$\bm{X}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}$ is.

For horizontality, put
$\bm{S}(\tau) := \bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{X}(\tau)-\bm{X}(\tau)^T\bm{A}_{\tau}$,
which by part 3 of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical} vanishes
precisely when $\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau),\beta(\tau)}$ is horizontal at
$\tilde{\gamma}(\tau)$.
Using $\dot{\bm{A}}_{\tau} = \bm{V}$,
$\dot{\bm{X}} = \bm{A}_{\tau}\bm{\Omega}$ and
$\bm{\Omega}^T = -\bm{\Omega}$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:S_dot}
  \dot{\bm{S}}(\tau)
  = \bm{V}^T\bm{X} + \bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{A}_{\tau}\bm{\Omega}
  - \bm{\Omega}^T\bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{A}_{\tau} - \bm{X}^T\bm{V}
  = \bracket{\bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{A}_{\tau}\bm{\Omega}
  + \bm{\Omega}\bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{A}_{\tau}}
  - \bracket{\bm{X}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}} = \bm{0},
\end{equation}
the last equality being exactly \eqref{eq:lyapunov} for
$\bm{\Omega} = \bm{\Omega}(\tau)$.
Since $\bm{S}(\tau_0) = \bm{0}$ by hypothesis, $\bm{S} \equiv \bm{0}$.

For the norm, the horizontality just proved makes
$\bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{X}(\tau)$ symmetric, so

\begin{equation*}
  \frac{d}{d\tau}\squared{\tr\bracket{\bm{X}\bm{X}^T}+\beta^2}
  = 2\tr\bracket{\bm{X}^T\dot{\bm{X}}} + 0
  = 2\tr\bracket{\bm{X}^T\bm{A}_{\tau}\bm{\Omega}}
  = 2\tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{A}_{\tau}^T\bm{X}}\bm{\Omega}} = 0,
\end{equation*}
the last equality because the trace of the product of a symmetric and a
skew-symmetric matrix vanishes.

\textbf{3.} Let $(\bm{X}_0,\beta_0) \in H_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau_0)}$ and
let $(\bm{X},\beta)$ be the solution of \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} it
determines.
By part 2 the pair $\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau),\beta(\tau)}$ is horizontal at
$\tilde{\gamma}(\tau)$, hence is the horizontal lift of

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{x}(\tau) := \mathrm{d}\pi_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau)}
  \bracket{\bracket{\bm{X}(\tau),\beta(\tau)}},
\end{equation*}
and its derivative $\bracket{\bm{A}_{\tau}\bm{\Omega}(\tau),\,0}$ is
vertical by Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical}, $\bm{\Omega}(\tau)$
being skew-symmetric.
The curve $\tilde{\gamma}$ has horizontal velocity at every time by part
1, so Lemma~\ref{thm:submersion_derivative} applies and gives
$D_{\tau}\bm{x} = 0$: the field $\bm{x}$ is parallel along $\gamma$.
Existence and uniqueness of the parallel field with a prescribed value at
$\tau_0$ (\citealp{Lee_2018}, Theorem 4.32) identify $\bm{x}$ with the
parallel transport of $\bm{x}(\tau_0)$, which is \eqref{eq:Pi_flow}.
The map $\Pi(\gamma)_{\tau_0}^{\tau_1}$ is linear because
\eqref{eq:transport_ODE} is; it is an isometry by the conservation of the
norm in part 2 together with the fact that each
$\iota_{\tilde{\gamma}(\tau)}$ is one; and
$\Pi(\gamma)_{\tau_1}^{\tau_0}$ inverts it, by uniqueness.

Parallel transport is unchanged if $\gamma$ is reparametrized.
Indeed, let $\phi : [a,b] \to [0,1]$ be a diffeomorphism.
The curve $\tilde{\gamma} \circ \phi$ lies over $\gamma \circ \phi$ and
has velocity $\dot{\phi}(\varsigma)\,(\bm{V},c)$, horizontal at every
time; and if $\tilde{\bm{x}}$ is the horizontal lift of a field $\bm{x}$
along $\gamma$, then $\tilde{\bm{x}} \circ \phi$ is the horizontal lift of
$\bm{x} \circ \phi$ along $\gamma \circ \phi$, with derivative
$\dot{\phi} \cdot \bracket{\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}} \circ \phi}$, which is
vertical exactly when $\dot{\tilde{\bm{x}}} \circ \phi$ is.
By the criterion closing Lemma~\ref{thm:submersion_derivative}, the field
$\bm{x} \circ \phi$ is parallel along $\gamma \circ \phi$ if and only if
$\bm{x}$ is parallel along $\gamma$.
Together with the uniqueness statement of part 1, this makes
$\Pi(\gamma)_1^0$ a function of the pair
$\bracket{[\bm{A},s],\ [\bm{B},t]}$ alone, so \eqref{eq:Pi_def} is well
posed.

For \eqref{eq:Pi_formula}, apply the above with $\tau_0 = 1$,
$\tau_1 = 0$ and $(\bm{X}_0,\beta_0) = (\bm{Y},\beta)$, which is
horizontal at $\tilde{\gamma}(1)$ by \eqref{eq:z_lift}: the second
component of \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} stays equal to $\beta$, the first is
\eqref{eq:transport_backward}, and part 2 supplies existence, uniqueness,
linearity in $(\bm{Y},\beta)$, smoothness in $(\bm{A},\bm{V})$ and the
preservation of the norm.
If $[\bm{B},t] = [\bm{A},s]$, then $\bm{V} = \bm{0}$ and $c = 0$, so
\eqref{eq:transport_backward} reads $\dot{\bm{X}} = \bm{0}$ and
$\bm{X}(0) = \bm{Y}$, whence
$\Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{A},s]} = \mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{A},s)} \circ
\iota_{(\bm{A},s)} = \text{id}$.
Lastly $\bm{V}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{V} = \bm{0}$, so the constant field
$\bm{X} \equiv \bm{V}$ solves \eqref{eq:transport_backward} with
$\bm{Y} = \bm{V}$; since
$\iota_{\tilde{\gamma}(1)}\bracket{\dot{\gamma}(1)} = (\bm{V},c)$, this
gives $\Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{B},t]}\bracket{\dot{\gamma}(1)}
= \mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{A},s)}\bracket{(\bm{V},c)} = \bm{v}$.
\end{proof}

Three features of \eqref{eq:Pi_formula} deserve to be isolated.
The scalar component is transported trivially, $\dot{\beta} = 0$: the
factor of $\Theta$ carrying $\sigma^2$ is a flat direction on which
$O(q)$ acts trivially, and it never mixes with the loading matrix.
When $q = 1$ the matrix $\bm{X}(\tau)^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}(\tau)$ is
skew-symmetric of size $1 \times 1$, hence zero, so
\eqref{eq:transport_backward} reads $\dot{\bm{X}} = \bm{0}$ and
$\Pi^{[\bm{A},s]}_{[\bm{B},t]}$ is the identity read in the horizontal
lifts; this is as it must be, since for $q = 1$ the group $O(1)$ is
discrete, $\pi$ is a local isometry and $M$ is flat.
For $q \geqslant 2$ there is no closed form, and what stands in the way is
the curvature of $(M,g)$: writing $\mathcal{K}_{\tau}$ for the linear
operator
$\bm{X} \mapsto \bm{A}_{\tau}\mathcal{L}_{\tau}
\bracket{\bm{X}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}}$ of $\mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$,
the terminal value problem \eqref{eq:transport_backward} is equivalent to

\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport_picard}
  \bm{X}(0) = \bm{Y}
  - \int_0^1 \mathcal{K}_{\sigma}\bracket{\bm{X}(\sigma)}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma,
  \qquad
  \left\|\mathcal{K}_{\sigma}\right\|
  \leqslant \frac{\|\bm{A}_{\sigma}\|\,\|\bm{V}\|_F}
  {\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}_{\sigma})^2},
\end{equation}
the operator norm being the one induced by $\|\, \cdot \,\|_F$, and the
bound following from
$\|\bm{X}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}\|_F \leqslant 2\|\bm{X}\|_F\|\bm{V}\|_F$
together with the observation that, in an orthonormal eigenbasis of
$\bm{A}_{\sigma}^T\bm{A}_{\sigma}$, solving \eqref{eq:lyapunov} divides
the $(i,j)$ entry by
$p_i+p_j \geqslant 2\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}_{\sigma})^2$.
So $\bm{X}(0)$ differs from $\bm{Y}$ by a quantity of the order of
$\|\bm{V}\|_F \leqslant d_M\bracket{[\bm{A},s],[\bm{B},t]}$: parallel
transport departs from the naive identification of the two horizontal
spaces by a term of the order of the distance travelled.
Controlling that term is the entire business of the next subsection.

\begin{definition}\label{def:transport}
For $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$ we write

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gamma_def}
  \Gamma_{\bm{u}} := \Pi^{\theta_0}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})} :
  T_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}M \longrightarrow T_{\theta_0}M
\end{equation}
for the map \eqref{eq:Pi_def} of
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport}, applied with
$[\bm{A},s] = \theta_0$ and $[\bm{B},t] = \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$.
By part 1 of that proposition the curve along which the transport is
taken is the radial geodesic
$\tau \mapsto \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\tau\bm{u})$ of the normal chart, and
it is the unique minimizing one; the subscript $\bm{u}$ therefore records
the second point through that chart rather than directly, which is the
form the estimates below need.
This is the map written $\Pi^{\psi(\theta)}_{\psi(\theta_{n,\bm{h}})}$ in
Definition III.5 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, in the case
$\psi = \text{id}$.
Concretely, in the notation \eqref{eq:iota_zero}, formula
\eqref{eq:Pi_formula} reads
$\Gamma_{\bm{u}}(\bm{z}) = \mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}
\bracket{\bracket{\bm{X}(0),\beta}}$, where
$(\bm{Y},\beta)
= \iota_{(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},\ \sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}})}(\bm{z})$ and
$\bm{X}$ solves \eqref{eq:transport_backward} with
$\bm{A}_{\tau} = \bm{W}_0+\tau\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}$ and
$\bm{V} = \bm{U}_{\bm{u}}$.
By Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport}, $\Gamma_{\bm{u}}$ is a linear
isometry and $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$; and, after both tangent spaces
are identified with subspaces of
$\mathbb{R}^{p \times q}\times\mathbb{R}$ through the horizontal lifts
$\iota$ and
$\iota_{(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},\ \sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}})}$, the map
$(\bm{u},\bm{z}) \mapsto \Gamma_{\bm{u}}(\bm{z})$ is smooth on
$B_{\rho} \times \bracket{\mathbb{R}^{p \times q}\times\mathbb{R}}$,
because $\bm{u} \mapsto \bracket{\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},b_{\bm{u}}}$ is linear
and $\bracket{\bm{A},\bm{V},\bm{Y},\beta} \mapsto
\bracket{\bm{X}(0),\beta}$ is smooth by part 3 of that proposition.
\end{definition}

\subsection*{The transport comparison}

Comparing errors measured at two different base points presupposes that
the logarithm may be taken at a \textit{moving} base point, and this is
the standard fact we record first: it holds provided both points stay in a
small enough neighbourhood of $\theta_0$.

\begin{lemma}[A uniformly normal neighbourhood]\label{thm:uniform_chart}
There exists $r \in \bracket{0,\frac{\rho}{4}}$ such that, writing

\begin{equation}\label{eq:V_def}
  \mathcal{V} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_r)
  \ \subseteq \
  \mathcal{V}^{\sharp} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_{2r})
  \ \subseteq \ \mathcal{U},
\end{equation}
the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For all $x, y \in \mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$ there is exactly one
  $\bm{v} \in \mathcal{E}_x$ with $\text{Exp}_x(\bm{v}) = y$; we denote it
  by $\text{Exp}_x^{-1}(y)$, and $\text{Exp}_x^{-1}(x) = \bm{0}$.
  \item The map
  $(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \mapsto
  \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}$
  is smooth on $B_{2r} \times B_{2r}$, read through the horizontal lift
  $\iota_{(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},\ \sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}})}$ as a map
  into $\mathbb{R}^{p \times q}\times\mathbb{R}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal{E} \subseteq TM$ be the set of pairs $(x,\bm{v})$ with
$\bm{v} \in \mathcal{E}_x$, the ball of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}.
Its radius at $x = [\bm{A},s]$ is $\min\bracket{\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}),s}$,
which is a well defined function on $M$ because the singular values of
$\bm{A}$ are unchanged by $\bm{A} \mapsto \bm{A}\bm{Q}$ with
$\bm{Q} \in O(q)$, and is continuous because its composition with the
submersion $\pi$ is.
Hence $\mathcal{E}$ is open in $TM$ and contains the zero section, and
$\text{Exp} : \mathcal{E} \to M$ is smooth, being affine in the horizontal
lift by \eqref{eq:exponential_map} and part 3 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:lyapunov_lemma}.
Consider

\begin{equation*}
  E : \mathcal{E} \longrightarrow M \times M,
  \qquad E(x,\bm{v}) := \bracket{x,\ \text{Exp}_x(\bm{v})} .
\end{equation*}
Its differential at $(\theta_0,\bm{0})$ is invertible: in the splitting
$T_{(\theta_0,\bm{0})}TM \cong T_{\theta_0}M \oplus T_{\theta_0}M$ it is
$(\bm{a},\bm{b}) \mapsto (\bm{a},\bm{a}+\bm{b})$, because
$\mathrm{d}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}}_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$.
The inverse function theorem therefore gives open sets
$\mathcal{N} \ni (\theta_0,\bm{0})$ in $\mathcal{E}$ and
$\mathcal{O} \ni (\theta_0,\theta_0)$ in $M \times M$ such that
$E : \mathcal{N} \to \mathcal{O}$ is a diffeomorphism.
This is the usual construction of uniformly normal neighbourhoods
(\citealp{Lee_2018}, Theorem 5.14 and its proof); note that completeness of
$M$, which fails here, is nowhere used, only the openness of the domain
$\mathcal{E}$ of $\text{Exp}$.
Writing $E^{-1}(x,y) = (x,\Xi(x,y))$, the map $\Xi$ is smooth on
$\mathcal{O}$ and satisfies $\text{Exp}_x\bracket{\Xi(x,y)} = y$ and
$\Xi(x,x) = \bm{0}$.
Since $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ is continuous and
$(\theta_0,\theta_0) \in \mathcal{O}$, we may choose
$r \in \bracket{0,\frac{\rho}{4}}$ so small that
$\mathcal{V}^{\sharp} \times \mathcal{V}^{\sharp} \subseteq \mathcal{O}$.

For part 1, existence is $\text{Exp}_x\bracket{\Xi(x,y)} = y$ together
with $\Xi(x,y) \in \mathcal{E}_x$, and uniqueness is the injectivity of
$\text{Exp}_x$ on $\mathcal{E}_x$, which is part 2 of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}; so
$\text{Exp}_x^{-1} = \Xi(x,\, \cdot \,)$ unambiguously, and
$\text{Exp}_x^{-1}(x) = \bm{0}$ because $\Xi(x,x) = \bm{0}$.
Part 2 follows by composing $\Xi$ with the smooth map
$(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \mapsto
\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u}),\ \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}$
and with the horizontal lift, which is smooth in its base point by part 3
of Lemma~\ref{thm:lyapunov_lemma}.
\end{proof}

We fix such an $r$ once and for all, together with $\mathcal{V}$ and
$\mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$ as in \eqref{eq:V_def}.
On $\mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$ the map $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}^{-1}$ in the sense
of part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart} agrees with the logarithmic map
\eqref{eq:logarithmic_def} of Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}, both
being inverses of the injective map $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ on $B_{\rho}$;
no ambiguity can therefore arise from the notation.
Part 1 of the lemma says exactly that $y \in U_x$ whenever
$x,y \in \mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$, so part 1 of
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} applies to every such pair: any
two points of $\mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$ are joined by a unique minimizing
geodesic, and the transport $\Pi^x_y$ of \eqref{eq:Pi_def} is defined
throughout $\mathcal{V}^{\sharp} \times \mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$.

Definition III.5 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} compares
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\hat{\theta}_n)$, transported back to
$T_{\theta_0}M$ by $\Pi^{\theta_0}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, with
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\theta}_n)$.
On a flat space the two would differ by exactly the vector joining the two
base points; on $M$ they differ by that vector plus a curvature
correction, and the whole content of this subsection is that the
correction is quadratically small.
Both base points are read through the normal chart at $\theta_0$, which
turns the comparison into a function of two chart vectors.
Define, using part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart} and
Definition~\ref{def:transport},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:F_def}
  F : B_{2r} \times B_{2r} \longrightarrow T_{\theta_0}M,
  \qquad
  F(\bm{u},\bm{w}) := \Gamma_{\bm{u}}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}} .
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Transport comparison]\label{thm:transport_comparison}
There is a constant $C < \infty$, depending only on $\theta_0$ and $r$,
such that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport_comparison}
  \abs{F(\bm{u},\bm{w}) - (\bm{w}-\bm{u})}
  \leqslant C\,\abs{\bm{u}}\,\abs{\bm{w}-\bm{u}}
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_r .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By part 2 of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart} and
Definition~\ref{def:transport}, $F$ is smooth on
$B_{2r} \times B_{2r}$; put

\begin{equation*}
  R(\bm{u},\bm{w}) := F(\bm{u},\bm{w}) - (\bm{w}-\bm{u}),
\end{equation*}
a smooth $T_{\theta_0}M$-valued function on $B_{2r} \times B_{2r}$.
It vanishes on two slices:

\begin{itemize}
  \item $R(\bm{u},\bm{u}) = \bm{0}$ for every $\bm{u} \in B_{2r}$,
  because $\text{Exp}^{-1}_x(x) = \bm{0}$ and $\Gamma_{\bm{u}}$ is
  linear, so $F(\bm{u},\bm{u}) = \bm{0} = \bm{u}-\bm{u}$;
  \item $R(\bm{0},\bm{w}) = \bm{0}$ for every $\bm{w} \in B_{2r}$,
  because $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$ and
  $\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}
  = \bm{w}$, so $F(\bm{0},\bm{w}) = \bm{w} = \bm{w}-\bm{0}$.
\end{itemize}
Set

\begin{equation*}
  C := \sup_{(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \in \overline{B_r}\times\overline{B_r}}
  \left\| \partial_1\partial_2 R(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \right\| < \infty,
\end{equation*}
the norm being the one induced on bilinear maps
$T_{\theta_0}M \times T_{\theta_0}M \to T_{\theta_0}M$ by
$\abs{\, \cdot \,}$; the supremum is finite because
$\partial_1\partial_2R$ is continuous and
$\overline{B_r}\times\overline{B_r}$ is a compact subset of the open set
$B_{2r}\times B_{2r}$ on which $R$ is smooth.

Let $\bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_r$.
The ball $B_r$ being convex, the segment from $\bm{u}$ to $\bm{w}$ lies in
it, and the fundamental theorem of calculus in the second variable,
together with $R(\bm{u},\bm{u}) = \bm{0}$, gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:R_first_taylor}
  R(\bm{u},\bm{w}) = \int_0^1
  \partial_2R\bracket{\bm{u},\ \bm{u}+t(\bm{w}-\bm{u})}
  \squared{\bm{w}-\bm{u}}\,\mathrm{d}t .
\end{equation}
Fix $\bm{z} \in B_r$.
The segment from $\bm{0}$ to $\bm{u}$ also lies in $B_r$, and
$\partial_2R(\bm{0},\bm{z}) = \bm{0}$ because $R(\bm{0},\, \cdot \,)$
vanishes identically; the same argument in the first variable therefore
gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:R_second_taylor}
  \partial_2R(\bm{u},\bm{z})
  = \int_0^1 \partial_1\partial_2R(\varsigma\bm{u},\bm{z})[\bm{u}]
  \,\mathrm{d}\varsigma,
  \qquad \text{whence} \qquad
  \left\|\partial_2R(\bm{u},\bm{z})\right\| \leqslant C\abs{\bm{u}} .
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:R_second_taylor} into \eqref{eq:R_first_taylor}
yields \eqref{eq:transport_comparison}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:transport_scale}
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} is the only place where the curvature
of $(M,g)$ enters the asymptotic theory, and
\eqref{eq:transport_comparison} says exactly how much it costs.
In the applications below $\bm{u}$ is a local perturbation of size
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ and $\bm{w}-\bm{u}$ is an estimation error of size
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$, so the right hand side is
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1})$; after the multiplication by $\sqrt{n}$ that all
these statements carry, it is $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$ and
disappears.
The two vanishing slices used in the proof are the two trivial cases of
the comparison, namely no displacement of the base point and no error to
compare, and it is their combination, not either alone, that produces the
product $\abs{\bm{u}}\abs{\bm{w}-\bm{u}}$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{The estimation error in the chart}

Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} speaks of
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\theta}_n)$, which is defined only when
$\hat{\theta}_n$ lies in the normal neighbourhood.
The next proposition is the bookkeeping that makes this a random vector
defined everywhere, on an event whose probability tends to one, and
identifies its horizontal lift.

\begin{proposition}[The error vector]\label{thm:error_welldefined}
Define

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e_n_def}
  \mathcal{A}_n := \curly{\hat{\theta}_n \in \mathcal{V}},
  \qquad
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n := \begin{cases}
    \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\hat{\theta}_n}
      & \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n, \\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Then, almost surely, $\mathcal{A}_n$ holds for all $n$ large enough; in
particular $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$, and the same
holds with $\mathcal{V}$ replaced by $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_{\epsilon})$
for any $\epsilon \in (0,r]$.
On $\mathcal{A}_n$ the horizontal lift of $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ is the
\textit{aligned error}

\begin{equation}\label{eq:aligned_error}
  \hat{\bm{e}}_n := \iota\bracket{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}
  = \bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n - \bm{W}_0,\ \hat{\sigma}_n^2-\sigma_0^2},
  \qquad
  \bm{Q}_n := \bm{Q}\bracket{\bm{W}_0,\hat{\bm{W}}_n} \in O(q),
\end{equation}
with $\bm{Q}(\, \cdot \,,\, \cdot \,)$ the orthogonal matrix
\eqref{eq:Q_A_B_def}.
Consequently Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} may be read off the
representatives: with $\bm{Z} \sim N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$ on
$T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n-\bm{W}_0,\
  \hat{\sigma}_n^2-\sigma_0^2}
  \rightsquigarrow \iota(\bm{Z})
  \qquad \text{in } H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)},
\end{equation}
the left hand side being $\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{e}}_n$ on $\mathcal{A}_n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_{\epsilon})$ is an open neighbourhood of
$\theta_0$ in $M$ for every $\epsilon \in (0,\rho]$, by part 2 of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}; since $\hat{\theta}_n \to \theta_0$
almost surely by Theorem~\ref{thm:consistency}, almost every realization
eventually enters it.
The identity \eqref{eq:aligned_error} is \eqref{eq:logarithmic_def} applied
with $[\bm{A},s] = \theta_0$ and $[\bm{B},t] = \hat{\theta}_n$, together
with the fact that $\mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$ and $\iota$ are
mutually inverse on horizontal vectors.
Finally, Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} gives
$\sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\theta}_n)
\rightsquigarrow \bm{Z}$, the convention on $\mathcal{A}_n^c$ being
harmless because two random vectors that agree on events of probability
tending to one have the same weak limits; applying the continuous mapping
theorem to the linear isometry $\iota$ and using
\eqref{eq:aligned_error} gives \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:MLE_CLT_comments}
Three comments on Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} orient everything
that follows.

\textbf{1. It is an intrinsic statement.}
The quantity that converges is the point $\hat{\theta}_n$ of $M$, read
through the logarithm at $\theta_0$.
The orthogonal alignment $\bm{Q}_n$ appearing in
\eqref{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes} is not an ad hoc Procrustes device
introduced to make a limit exist: by
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} it \textit{is} the logarithmic map
of the first chapter, written in a representative.

\textbf{2. It concerns one law.}
The expansion \eqref{eq:asc_linearity} and the ensuing weak convergence
hold under the single law $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, and
Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} will show that the local regularity proved below
does not follow from them, not even if they are assumed at every other
point of $M$.

\textbf{3. No assumption is made on the multiplicities of the
$\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)$.}
The result is valid when some, or all, of
$\sigma_1(\bm{W}_0),\ldots,\sigma_q(\bm{W}_0)$ coincide, a case in which
the individual eigenvectors of $\bm{S}_n$ are \textit{not} asymptotically
normal.
There is no contradiction: an individual eigenvector is not a function of
the point $\hat{\theta}_n \in M$, whereas everything in
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} is.
Section~\ref{sec:fisher_explicit} makes this quantitative by exhibiting
coordinates in which the limiting covariance is computed without ever
dividing by a difference of singular values.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Coordinates, and the local experiments}

We keep the $g_{\theta_0}$-orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ of
$T_{\theta_0}M$, the identification $T_{\theta_0}M \cong \mathbb{R}^d$ it
provides, the matrix $\bm{G}$ with entries
$\bm{G}_{jk} = G(\bm{e}_j,\bm{e}_k)$ and the coordinate vector
$\nabla l(\bm{x};\theta_0) \in \mathbb{R}^d$ of the score, with entries
$s(\bm{x};\theta_0)(\bm{e}_j)$, all fixed in
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} and its proof.
Under this identification the Riemannian norm $\abs{\, \cdot \,}$ becomes
the Euclidean norm, and bilinearity gives
$G(\bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2) = \bm{h}_1^T\bm{G}\bm{h}_2$ and
$s(\bm{x};\theta_0)(\bm{h}) = \nabla l(\bm{x};\theta_0)^T\bm{h}$; the
matrix $\bm{G}$ is positive definite by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}.
We abbreviate the normalized score

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Delta_n_def}
  \bm{\Delta}_n := \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n
  \nabla l(\bm{X}_i;\theta_0) \in \mathbb{R}^d ,
\end{equation}
so that \eqref{eq:asc_linearity_in_proof} reads

\begin{equation}\label{eq:AL_recalled}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n
  = \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0} .
\end{equation}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} the vectors
$\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0),\nabla l(\bm{X}_2;\theta_0),\ldots$ are
independent, identically distributed, centred and square integrable with
covariance matrix $\bm{G}$, so the multivariate central limit theorem
gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:score_CLT}
  \bm{\Delta}_n \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0} .
\end{equation}
Finally, for $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ and $n$ large enough that
$\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}} \in B_r$ we write

\begin{equation}\label{eq:local_alternative}
  \theta_{n,\bm{h}} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}
  \bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}} \in \mathcal{V},
  \qquad \theta_{n,\bm{0}} = \theta_0,
\end{equation}
for the \textit{geodesic local alternative} in the direction $\bm{h}$.
In this notation the local asymptotic normality expansion
\eqref{eq:LAN_expansion} of Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} reads

\begin{equation}\label{eq:LAN_coordinates}
  \Lambda_n(\bm{h}) := \sum_{i=1}^n \log
  \frac{p\bracket{\bm{X}_i;\ \theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}{p(\bm{X}_i;\theta_0)}
  = \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h} - \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}
  + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
\end{equation}
because the score is a linear functional, so that
$\sum_{i=1}^n s\bracket{\bm{X}_i;\theta_0}
\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}} = \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}$.

\subsection*{Local regularity}

We can now state the definition.
Given an $M$-valued estimator sequence $T_n$ we put, with
$\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ the geodesic local alternative
\eqref{eq:local_alternative},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:errors_def}
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] := \begin{cases}
    \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(T_n) & \text{on } \curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}},\\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
  \qquad
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n] := \begin{cases}
    \Gamma_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
    \bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(T_n)}
      & \text{on } \curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}},\\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
both taking values in the single vector space $T_{\theta_0}M$; the second
is well defined as soon as $\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}} \in B_r$, by part 1 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}.
For the maximum likelihood estimator the first of these is exactly the
vector $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ of \eqref{eq:e_n_def}, and we keep that notation
for it.
Following \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} we call an estimator sequence $T_n$
\textit{localizable at $\theta_0$} when
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}(T_n \in \mathcal{V}) \to 1$, which by
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} the maximum likelihood estimator
is; only for such sequences do the quantities \eqref{eq:errors_def} carry
any information in the limit.

\begin{definition}\label{def:regular}
A localizable estimator sequence $T_n$ is \textit{locally regular at
$\theta_0$ with limit law $L$}, where $L$ is a Borel probability measure on
$T_{\theta_0}M$, if for every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$

\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularity}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n] \rightsquigarrow L
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} ,
\end{equation}
the limit $L$ being the same for every $\bm{h}$.
This is Definition III.5 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} with
$\psi = \text{id}$ and $\Psi = M$: by \eqref{eq:Gamma_def} the map
$\Gamma_{\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}}$ of \eqref{eq:errors_def} is their
$\Pi^{\theta_0}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, so \eqref{eq:regularity} is their
display (16) verbatim.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Contiguity]\label{thm:contiguity}
For every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ the sequences
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ and $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ are
mutually contiguous.
Consequently, if a sequence of random vectors is $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$,
respectively $O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$, under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, then it
is so under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ as well.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:LAN_weak_convergence} of Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} the
log-likelihood ratio $\Lambda_n(\bm{h})$ of \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates}
converges in distribution under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ to
$Z \sim N\bracket{-\frac{1}{2}G(\bm{h},\bm{h}),\ G(\bm{h},\bm{h})}$, and
$\mathbb{E}\squared{e^{Z}}
= e^{-\frac{1}{2}G(\bm{h},\bm{h})+\frac{1}{2}G(\bm{h},\bm{h})} = 1$, so
Le Cam's first lemma in the form of Example 6.5 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998} gives mutual contiguity.
The transfer of $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ is immediate from the definition of
contiguity.
For $O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$, suppose $\bm{Z}_n$ were not bounded in
probability under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$: there would be
$\varepsilon > 0$, a subsequence $n_k$ and constants $M_k \to \infty$ with
$\mathbb{P}^{n_k}_{\theta_{n_k,\bm{h}}}
\bracket{\abs{\bm{Z}_{n_k}} > M_k} > \varepsilon$ for all $k$, whereas
$\mathbb{P}^{n_k}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\abs{\bm{Z}_{n_k}}>M_k} \to 0$
because $\bm{Z}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ and
$M_k \to \infty$; contiguity makes these two incompatible.
\end{proof}

The next lemma is the bridge: on this manifold, regularity in the
intrinsic sense of Definition~\ref{def:regular} is the same thing as
regularity of the ordinary Euclidean kind, read in the normal chart at
$\theta_0$.
It is what allows the classical Euclidean theory to be applied verbatim
here.

\begin{lemma}[Chart form of regularity]\label{thm:chart_regularity}
Let $T_n$ be a localizable estimator sequence with
$\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$.
Then, for every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:chart_form}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n]
  = \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] - \bm{h} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} .
\end{equation}
Consequently $T_n$ is locally regular at $\theta_0$ with limit law $L$ if
and only if

\begin{equation}\label{eq:chart_regularity}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] - \bm{h} \rightsquigarrow L
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
  \text{ for every } \bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M .
\end{equation}
Moreover the boundedness hypothesis is automatic: every locally regular
sequence satisfies it.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\bm{h}$ and abbreviate $\hat{\bm{u}}_n := \hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]$,
$\bm{u}_n := \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}$ and
$\mathcal{B}_n := \curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}}$.
By hypothesis $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}(\mathcal{B}_n) \to 1$, hence
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{B}_n) \to 1$ by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}, and by the same lemma
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ as well.
On $\mathcal{B}_n$, and for $n$ large enough that $\bm{u}_n \in B_r$, we
have $T_n = \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$ with
$\hat{\bm{u}}_n \in B_r$, so \eqref{eq:F_def} and \eqref{eq:errors_def}
give

\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n]
  = \Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}\bracket{
  \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u}_n)}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)}}
  = F(\bm{u}_n,\hat{\bm{u}}_n) .
\end{equation*}
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} therefore gives, on
$\mathcal{B}_n$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison_applied}
  \abs{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n]
  - \bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}}
  = \sqrt{n}\,\abs{F(\bm{u}_n,\hat{\bm{u}}_n)
  - (\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{u}_n)}
  \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,C\,\frac{\abs{\bm{h}}}{\sqrt{n}}\,
  \abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  = \frac{C\abs{\bm{h}}}{\sqrt{n}}
  \abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}} .
\end{equation}
The right hand side is
$O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}} = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, and
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{B}_n^c) \to 0$, which proves
\eqref{eq:chart_form}.
The equivalence of \eqref{eq:regularity} and \eqref{eq:chart_regularity}
is then Slutsky's lemma.

For the last assertion, let $T_n$ be locally regular with limit law $L$ and
take $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ in \eqref{eq:regularity}: since
$\theta_{n,\bm{0}} = \theta_0$ and $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$, this
reads $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] \rightsquigarrow L$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, and a weakly convergent sequence is bounded in
probability by Prohorov's theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Local regularity of the maximum likelihood estimator]\label{thm:MLE_regular}
Let $1 \leqslant q < p$ and let $\theta_0 = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2] \in M$.
Then the maximum likelihood estimator $\hat{\theta}_n$ of
Section~\ref{sec:MLE} is locally regular at $\theta_0$ in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:regular}, with limit law

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_regular_limit}
  L = N\bracket{\bm{0},\ G^{-1}}
  \qquad \text{on } T_{\theta_0}M,
\end{equation}
which does not depend on $\bm{h}$.
Explicitly, for every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_regular_statement}
  \sqrt{n}\ \Gamma_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}\bracket{\hat{\theta}_n}}
  \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{0},\ G^{-1}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We work in the coordinates \eqref{eq:Delta_n_def} and fix
$\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$.

\textbf{Step 1: a single linear statistic drives everything.}
The two expansions \eqref{eq:AL_recalled} and \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates},
both valid under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, are linear in the
\textit{same} vector $\bm{\Delta}_n$ of \eqref{eq:Delta_n_def}, so no
separate joint central limit theorem is needed: by \eqref{eq:score_CLT},
$\bm{\Delta}_n \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G})$, and the continuous
mapping theorem applied to the linear map
$\bm{\delta} \mapsto \bracket{\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\delta},\
\bm{\delta}^T\bm{h}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}}$, together with
Slutsky's lemma, yields

\begin{equation}\label{eq:joint_convergence}
  \bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n,\ \Lambda_n(\bm{h})}
  \rightsquigarrow
  N\bracket{
  \begin{pmatrix} \bm{0}\\ -\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}\end{pmatrix},
  \begin{pmatrix} \bm{G}^{-1} & \bm{h} \\
  \bm{h}^T & \bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}\end{pmatrix}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0},
\end{equation}
the off-diagonal block being
$\text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta},\ \bm{\Delta}^T\bm{h}}
= \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G}\bm{h} = \bm{h}$ for
$\bm{\Delta} \sim N(\bm{0},\bm{G})$.

\textbf{Step 2: Le Cam's third lemma.}
The limit in \eqref{eq:joint_convergence} has exactly the form required by
Example 6.7 of \cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, the Gaussian case of Le Cam's
third lemma (Theorem 6.6 there): the last coordinate is the
log-likelihood ratio and is
$N\bracket{-\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h},\ \bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}}$,
with $\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h} = G(\bm{h},\bm{h})$.
The cited result therefore gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:third_lemma_conclusion}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} :
\end{equation}
the limit under the contiguous alternative is the limit under the null
shifted by the covariance $\bm{h}$ between the statistic and the
log-likelihood ratio.
Equivalently
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h} \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, a law free of $\bm{h}$.

\textbf{Step 3: from the chart to the transported error.}
The hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} hold for
$T_n = \hat{\theta}_n$: it is localizable by
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}, and
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ by Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}.
The equivalence \eqref{eq:chart_regularity} of that lemma therefore turns
\eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion} into
\eqref{eq:MLE_regular_statement}, with limit law $N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$,
which in the $g_{\theta_0}$-orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$
represents $N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:hodges}
It is worth being explicit about what \eqref{eq:MLE_regular_statement}
excludes, since Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} alone excludes nothing
of the kind.
Fix a point $\theta_* \in M$, known to the statistician, and let
$\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}$ be the Hodges-type modification of
$\hat{\theta}_n$ that replaces it by $\theta_*$ whenever
$\hat{\theta}_n \in \text{Exp}_{\theta_*}(B)$, with $B$ the ball of radius
$n^{-\frac{1}{4}}$ in $T_{\theta_*}M$, and leaves it unchanged otherwise.

For any fixed true parameter $\theta_0 \neq \theta_*$ the modification
occurs on an event of probability tending to zero, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:consistency}, so $\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}$
satisfies the exact analogue of
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} there.
For $\theta_0 = \theta_*$ it does better: since
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}
\ll n^{-\frac{1}{4}}$ by Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}, we have
$\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}} = \theta_*$ with probability tending to one, so
that
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n\squared{\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}}
\rightsquigarrow \delta_{\bm{0}}$, a limit strictly more concentrated than
$N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$.
This is the superefficiency phenomenon, and it shows that no bound
whatsoever can be extracted from pointwise statements of the type of
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} alone.

And yet, at $\theta_0 = \theta_*$ and $\bm{h} \neq \bm{0}$, the same
computation gives
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n\squared{\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}}
\rightsquigarrow \delta_{\bm{0}}$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ as well --- the estimator is pinned to
$\theta_*$ on an event of probability tending to one, since
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}
\ll n^{-\frac{1}{4}}$ under the alternative too, by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} --- so by \eqref{eq:chart_form} its transported
error converges to $\delta_{-\bm{h}}$, which \textit{does} depend on
$\bm{h}$.
Hence $\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}$ is superefficient at $\theta_*$ and is
not locally regular there.
Local regularity is precisely the statement that the asymptotics of
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} do not degrade as the truth is moved at
the scale $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$, and it is a strictly stronger property.
\end{remark}

\section{Consequences}\label{sec:consequences}

\subsection*{What local regularity adds}

Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} and Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}
end in the same limit law $N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$, and the second was
deduced from the first in three short steps; it is therefore worth saying
plainly, before anything is derived from it, what the second asserts that
the first does not.
The difference is not in the limit.
It is in the \textit{set of laws under which the limit is asserted}:
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} is a statement about the single
sequence $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, whereas Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}
is a statement about the whole shrinking family
$\curly{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} : \bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M}$ of
laws that no experiment of size $n$ can tell apart from it.
Everything in this section is a consequence of that one difference.

\textbf{Why the difference is not a technicality.}
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} says that $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$
and $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ are mutually contiguous: for any fixed
$\bm{h}$, no sequence of tests whose level under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ tends to zero can have power against
$\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ tending to one, and symmetrically with the two
parameters exchanged.
The qualification about the level is not decorative --- the test that
always rejects has power $1$ --- but it is exactly the qualification
under which a testing problem is nontrivial.
The statistician who has observed $\bm{X}_1,\ldots,\bm{X}_n$ is therefore
never in a position to assert that the truth is $\theta_0$ rather than
some $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$; the best that can ever be known is that the
truth lies within $O\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$ of the estimate, which is
exactly the resolution at which the family $\curly{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$
lives.
An asymptotic property that holds at $\theta_0$ but fails at
$\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ is thus \textit{empirically unverifiable}: it is
attached to a point that the data cannot single out.
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} is such a statement as it stands,
and Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} shows that it is not vacuously stable --- the
Hodges-type estimator constructed there satisfies the exact analogue of
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} at every point of $M$
\textit{other than} $\theta_*$, does strictly better than it at
$\theta_*$ itself, and is nevertheless worthless near $\theta_*$, because
the point at which its behaviour is anomalous moves with the sample size
in precisely the way the statistician cannot track.
Local regularity is the repair: it upgrades a claim indexed by a point to
a claim indexed by a neighbourhood of the resolution scale, and it is
exactly the strength needed, no more.
We now list what this buys.

\textbf{1. There is a lower bound at all.}
This is the largest single gain, and it is a gain of \textit{kind}, not
of degree.
From Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} alone \textit{no} optimality
statement about $\hat{\theta}_n$ can be extracted, at $\theta_0$ or
anywhere else: Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} exhibits a competitor whose limit
law at $\theta_*$ is $\delta_{\bm{0}}$, infinitely more concentrated than
$N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$ for every reasonable measure of concentration,
and the same device can be run at any finite set of points at once.
The class of all estimator sequences carries no nontrivial asymptotic
lower bound whatsoever.
Once attention is restricted to the locally regular ones, the
H\'ajek--Le~Cam convolution theorem in the manifold form of
Theorem III.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} applies to the present model ---
its hypotheses are Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} and Definition~\ref{def:regular},
both verified here --- and yields, for every locally regular $T_n$ with
limit law $L$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:convolution}
  L = N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}} * \mu
  \qquad \text{for some Borel probability measure } \mu
  \text{ on } T_{\theta_0}M .
\end{equation}
Nothing below depends on the manifold form of the theorem being
available.
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} turns Definition~\ref{def:regular} into
ordinary Euclidean regularity read in the normal chart, where
\eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} exhibits a locally asymptotically normal
sequence of experiments with nonsingular information matrix $\bm{G}$; the
classical convolution theorem, Chapter 8 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, applies to that sequence and returns
\eqref{eq:convolution} directly.
Either way, every locally regular limit law is the law of
$\bm{Z}+\bm{N}$ with $\bm{Z} \sim N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$ independent
of $\bm{N} \sim \mu$: relative to the limit of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}, an independent noise term has been
\textit{added}, never subtracted.
Two consequences of \eqref{eq:convolution} deserve to be named
separately, because they are the statements a practitioner actually uses;
a third item records, for contrast and for orientation only, the
intrinsic Cram\'er--Rao inequality that the convolution theorem replaces
here.

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textit{Efficiency.}
  Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} exhibits $\hat{\theta}_n$ as the case
  $\mu = \delta_{\bm{0}}$ of \eqref{eq:convolution}, so the maximum
  likelihood estimator is asymptotically efficient in the class of
  locally regular estimators of $\theta_0 \in M$.
  This is Theorem V.1(i) of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, whose proof consists
  of exactly the verification carried out in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}.
  \item \textit{A risk bound for every bowl-shaped loss.}
  Call $\ell : T_{\theta_0}M \to [0,\infty)$ bowl-shaped if its sublevel
  sets $\curly{\ell \leqslant c}$ are closed, convex and symmetric about
  $\bm{0}$; equivalently, if $\ell$ is nonnegative, lower semicontinuous
  and quasiconvex with $\ell(-\bm{v}) = \ell(\bm{v})$.
  Anderson's lemma applied to \eqref{eq:convolution} gives
  $\mathbb{E}\squared{\ell\bracket{\bm{Z}+\bm{N}}}
  \geqslant \mathbb{E}\squared{\ell(\bm{Z})}$, and the portmanteau
  theorem for nonnegative lower semicontinuous functions --- this is
  where the sublevel sets are required to be \textit{closed} --- turns
  the convergence
  $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] \rightsquigarrow L$ into

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:risk_bound}
    \liminf_n \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}}
    \squared{\ell\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]}}
    \geqslant \mathbb{E}\squared{\ell(\bm{Z})}
    \qquad \text{for every locally regular } T_n ,
  \end{equation}
  and equally with $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ replaced by any
  $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, the limit law being the same under
  all of them.
  See Chapter 8 of \cite{van_der_Vaart_1998} for the Euclidean argument,
  which Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} imports verbatim.
  Squared error is the case $\ell = \abs{\, \cdot \,}^2$, but so is
  $\ell = \bm{1}_{A^c}$ for a fixed closed convex symmetric $A$, that is
  the probability of missing a fixed region, which is what a confidence
  statement is about.
  \item \textit{The asymptotic Cram\'er--Rao bound.}
  The intrinsic Cram\'er--Rao inequality on a parameter manifold,
  Lemma III.2 and Corollary III.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, bounds
  below the covariance of the residual of an \textit{unbiased} estimator
  in the sense of their Definition III.3, and carries at finite $n$ two
  extra terms built from the curvature tensor of $(M,g)$ which have no
  Euclidean counterpart; by the linearity of the curvature operator
  recorded there, those terms are $O\bracket{n^{-1}}$ while the main term
  $G^{-1}$ is $O(1)$, so that the bound reduces asymptotically to the
  curvature-free $G^{-1}$.
  We use it nowhere.
  Converting it into a statement about $\hat{\theta}_n$ would require an
  unbiasedness hypothesis, which nothing here gives any reason to expect
  of the maximum likelihood estimator at finite $n$, and the convergence
  $\text{Cov}\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n} \to \bm{G}^{-1}$, which does
  \textit{not} follow from Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}: weak
  convergence to $N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}}$ controls second moments
  only through the $\liminf$ inequality \eqref{eq:risk_bound}, and
  upgrading that to convergence needs the uniform integrability of
  $\abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^2$, which is nowhere established here and
  is not innocuous, $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ being defined by truncation to
  $\bm{0}$ off $\mathcal{A}_n$ in \eqref{eq:e_n_def}.
  The comparison we do make is the one the convolution theorem supplies,
  namely \eqref{eq:convolution} and \eqref{eq:risk_bound}, which are
  statements about laws and need no moment hypothesis at all.
\end{itemize}

None of this is available to someone holding
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} alone, for the simple reason that
\eqref{eq:convolution} is false without the word \textit{regular}: the
Hodges sequence has $L = \delta_{\bm{0}}$ at $\theta_*$, and
$\delta_{\bm{0}}$ is not of the form $N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}} * \mu$,
such a convolution having a nonsingular Gaussian factor and hence a
density.

\textbf{2. The influence operator is pinned down by a pointwise
identity.}
Asymptotic linearity says that the estimator is, to first order, an
average of a fixed function of the data; it places no constraint at all
on \textit{which} function.
Local regularity places exactly one constraint, and it is a constraint
that can be checked at a single point of the model, with no asymptotics
at all.
Proposition~\ref{thm:regular_IF} below states it: an asymptotically
linear sequence is locally regular \textit{if and only if} its influence
operator $\text{IF}'$ satisfies

\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_normalization}
  \mathbb{E}\squared{s(\bm{X};\theta_0)(\bm{z})\ \text{IF}'(\bm{X})}
  = \bm{z}
  \qquad \text{for every } \bm{z} \in T_{\theta_0}M ,
\end{equation}
that is, if and only if the cross-covariance between the influence
operator and the score is the identity operator of $T_{\theta_0}M$.
This is the specialization to $\psi = \text{id}$ of the calculus of
influence functions of Lemma III.3 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}; the
proposition below adds the converse, which is what makes
\eqref{eq:IF_normalization} usable as a criterion.
The gain is concrete.
A statistician who has computed an influence operator for a competing
estimator, by whatever means, may decide whether that estimator is
locally regular by evaluating one expectation under the true law, and may
then read off its asymptotic covariance and compare it with $G^{-1}$ ---
and, by part 2 of that proposition, the comparison always goes the same
way.
Without local regularity the influence operator is merely \textit{some}
centred square integrable function subject to no normalization, and no
comparison between two estimators is possible at all.

\textbf{3. The estimator becomes equivariant in the limit experiment.}
The expansion \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} of Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN}
says that the local experiments
$\bracket{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} : \bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M}$
converge, in Le Cam's sense, to the Gaussian shift experiment
$\bracket{N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}} : \bm{h} \in \mathbb{R}^d}$, in
which one observes a single Gaussian vector whose mean is the unknown
local parameter and whose covariance is known (\citealp{van_der_Vaart_1998},
Chapter 9).
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} identifies the behaviour of
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ at the \textit{single} point $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$
of that limit experiment, which is no more informative than knowing the
value of a function at one point of its domain.
Equation \eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion} identifies it at every
$\bm{h}$ at once, and the answer, $N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}}$, is
exactly the law of the identity estimator $\bm{y} \mapsto \bm{y}$ in the
limit experiment: in the limit, $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ \textit{is} the
natural shift-equivariant estimator of the local parameter.
Local regularity is the assertion that the map sending $\bm{h}$ to the
law of $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$
converges to an equivariant family, and equivariance is the property that
transfers the optimality theory of the limit experiment --- where it is a
finite dimensional Gaussian computation --- back to the sequence.
This is the structural reason behind item 1: \eqref{eq:convolution} is
the image, under the convergence of experiments, of the elementary fact
that an equivariant estimator of a Gaussian mean cannot improve on the
observation itself.

\textbf{4. The property survives smooth transformation.}
Let $\varphi$ be a smooth map from a neighbourhood of $\theta_0$ in $M$
into a fixed finite dimensional vector space; shrinking the radius $r$ of
\eqref{eq:V_def} if necessary, we may assume that $\varphi$ is defined on
all of $\mathcal{V}$.
As in \eqref{eq:errors_def}, the random vector $\varphi(T_n)$ is to be
read with the convention that it is set to a fixed value, say
$\varphi(\theta_0)$, off the event $\curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}}$; the
convention is harmless because that event has probability tending to one
under every $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, by localizability and
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}, and two random vectors agreeing on events of
probability tending to one have the same weak limits.
With that convention, if $T_n$ is locally regular at $\theta_0$ with
limit law $L$, then $\varphi(T_n)$ satisfies

\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta_regular}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\varphi(T_n)-\varphi\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}
  \rightsquigarrow \bracket{\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\theta_0}}_{\#}L
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}},
\end{equation}
with a limit free of $\bm{h}$.
This is the delta method applied in the normal chart to
\eqref{eq:chart_regularity}.
Indeed, put $\tilde{\varphi} := \varphi \circ \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$,
smooth on $B_r$ and with differential $\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\theta_0}$ at
$\bm{0}$ because
$\mathrm{d}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}}_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$; Taylor's
theorem, with the second derivative of $\tilde{\varphi}$ bounded on the
compact set $\overline{B_{r/2}}$, supplies a constant $c < \infty$ with

\begin{equation*}
  \abs{\tilde{\varphi}(\bm{a})-\tilde{\varphi}(\bm{b})
  -\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\theta_0}(\bm{a}-\bm{b})}
  \leqslant c\bracket{\abs{\bm{a}}^2+\abs{\bm{b}}^2}
  \qquad \text{for } \bm{a},\bm{b} \in \overline{B_{r/2}} .
\end{equation*}
Apply this with $\bm{a} = \hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]$ and
$\bm{b} = \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}$, both in $\overline{B_{r/2}}$ with
probability tending to one, and multiply by $\sqrt{n}$: the left hand
side of \eqref{eq:delta_regular} equals
$\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\theta_0}\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]-\bm{h}}$
up to a remainder bounded by
$c\,n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bracket{\abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]}^2
+\abs{\bm{h}}^2}$, which is $O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$
because $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ by Lemmas~\ref{thm:contiguity} and
\ref{thm:chart_regularity}.
Part 3 of Corollary~\ref{thm:Sigma_CLT} below is the instance
$\varphi = \bm{\Sigma}$, carried out in full and with an exact remainder,
and Remark~\ref{rmk:what_is_added} records that part 3 is the only part
of that corollary requiring anything from Section~\ref{sec:regularity}.
The point is that \eqref{eq:delta_regular} has no analogue starting from
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}: the delta method applied to a
pointwise central limit theorem returns a pointwise central limit
theorem, and a family of pointwise statements indexed by $\theta$ carries
no information about how they fit together, as Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges}
demonstrates.
Local regularity is stable under the operations one performs on
estimators in practice; pointwise asymptotic normality is not.

\textbf{5. Confidence sets that cover what they claim to cover.}
This is the operational payoff.
Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence} constructs the Wald region
$\mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha} \subseteq M$ and shows that its coverage tends
to $1-\alpha$ along every sequence of local alternatives, not merely at
$\theta_0$.
Remark~\ref{rmk:coverage_uniform} shows what the failure looks like when
regularity is absent: the Hodges region covers $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ with
limiting probability $1$ for small $\bm{h}$ and $0$ for large $\bm{h}$,
and $1-\alpha$ for no $\bm{h}$ whatsoever, $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ included.
Since the statistician does not know $\bm{h}$ and, by contiguity, cannot
come to know it, a region whose coverage depends on $\bm{h}$ reports a
confidence level that is not the level it achieves.

The rest of the section carries this out.
The next subsection collects the three elementary facts that the
arguments need.
The two subsections after it prove the characterization announced in item
2 and answer the question raised by item 3, namely in what sense
$\hat{\theta}_n$ is asymptotically normal \textit{on the manifold}.
The last two contain the consequences themselves: the confidence sets of
item 5, and the instance of item 4 at the level of the fitted covariance
matrix.

\subsection*{Three preliminaries}

The first preliminary is that the covariance matrix is a
\textit{quadratic} function of the local parameter, exactly and with no
remainder, which is what makes every delta method argument below an
identity rather than an approximation.
Throughout the section we write
$\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} := \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}$
for the covariance matrix at the point of $M$ with normal coordinate
$\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:Sigma_u}
Let $\mathcal{D}_0 : T_{\theta_0}M \to \text{Sym}(p)$ be the linear map

\begin{equation}\label{eq:D_0_def}
  \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) := D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)
  = \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T + \bm{W}_0\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T
  + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p
\end{equation}
of \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional}.
Then, for every $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$, one has the \textit{exact}
identity

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic}
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}
  = \bm{\Sigma}_0 + \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{u})
  + \bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}^T ,
\end{equation}
with no remainder.
Moreover, with $r$ the radius fixed in \eqref{eq:V_def}, there are
constants $0 < c_1 \leqslant c_2 < \infty$, depending only on $\theta_0$
and $r$, such that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:spectral_bounds}
  c_1\bm{I}_p \preceq \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} \preceq c_2\bm{I}_p
  \qquad \text{for every } \bm{u} \in \overline{B_r} .
\end{equation}
In particular $\bm{u} \mapsto \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}$ is smooth and
bounded on $\overline{B_r}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:exponential_map} and \eqref{eq:Sigma_Phi_def},

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}
  = \bracket{\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}}\bracket{\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}}^T
  + \bracket{\sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}}}\bm{I}_p
  = \bm{\Sigma}_0 + \bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\bm{W}_0^T + \bm{W}_0\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}^T
  + b_{\bm{u}}\bm{I}_p + \bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}^T,
\end{equation*}
which is \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic}.
For the bounds, the first summand of the middle expression is positive
semidefinite, and $\abs{b_{\bm{u}}} \leqslant \abs{\bm{u}} \leqslant r
< \rho \leqslant \sigma_0^2$ by \eqref{eq:lift_properties} and
\eqref{eq:rho_def}; hence
$\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} \succeq \bracket{\sigma_0^2-r}\bm{I}_p$ and we may
take $c_1 := \sigma_0^2-r > 0$.
Similarly $\|\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\| \leqslant \|\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\|_F \leqslant r$,
so that $\|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}\| \leqslant
\bracket{\|\bm{W}_0\|+r}^2+\sigma_0^2+r =: c_2$.
\end{proof}

The second preliminary is the general notion of asymptotic linearity, of
which \eqref{eq:asc_linearity} is the instance proved in
Section~\ref{sec:MLE}.
Naming it lets us record that the influence operator of a sequence is
determined by the sequence, so that \eqref{eq:influence_function} is
\textit{the} influence operator of $\hat{\theta}_n$ and not merely one of
them.

\begin{definition}[Asymptotic linearity]\label{def:asymptotic_linearity}
A localizable estimator sequence $T_n$ is \textit{asymptotically linear at
$\theta_0$ with influence operator}
$\text{IF}' : \mathbb{R}^p \to T_{\theta_0}M$ if $\text{IF}'$ is Borel
measurable, $\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1)$ is centred and square integrable, and

\begin{equation}\label{eq:asymptotic_linearity}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]
  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n \text{IF}'(\bm{X}_i) + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0},
\end{equation}
with $\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]$ the chart error of \eqref{eq:errors_def}.
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} says that $\hat{\theta}_n$ is
asymptotically linear in this sense, with influence operator
$\text{IF}$ of \eqref{eq:influence_function}.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Uniqueness of the influence operator]\label{thm:IF_unique}
If $\text{IF}'$ and $\text{IF}''$ both satisfy
\eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity} for the same estimator sequence $T_n$,
then $\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1) = \text{IF}''(\bm{X}_1)$ almost surely.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Subtracting the two expansions \eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity} gives
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{D}(\bm{X}_i) = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ with
$\bm{D} := \text{IF}'-\text{IF}''$, which is centred and square integrable
by Definition~\ref{def:asymptotic_linearity}.
The multivariate central limit theorem gives
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n\bm{D}(\bm{X}_i) \rightsquigarrow
N\bracket{\bm{0},\text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1)}}$, and a sequence
converging in distribution to a limit and also to $\bm{0}$ in probability
forces that limit to be $\delta_{\bm{0}}$; hence
$\text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1)} = \bm{0}$ and, $\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1)$
being centred, $\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1) = \bm{0}$ almost surely.
\end{proof}

The third preliminary is that the Fisher information operator may be
evaluated at a nearby point, and in particular at the estimator, without
losing smoothness or positive definiteness.
To compare the operators at two points we read them in one and the same
frame, transported along the radial geodesic.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:information_smooth}
For $\bm{u} \in B_r$ let $\bm{G}(\bm{u})$ be the $d \times d$ matrix

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_transported}
  \bm{G}(\bm{u})_{jk} := G_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}
  \bracket{\Gamma_{\bm{u}}^{-1}(\bm{e}_j),\ \Gamma_{\bm{u}}^{-1}(\bm{e}_k)},
  \qquad j,k = 1,\ldots,d,
\end{equation}
the matrix of the Fisher information operator at
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$ in the frame obtained by transporting
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ along the radial geodesic.
Then $\bm{u} \mapsto \bm{G}(\bm{u})$ is smooth on $B_r$, positive definite
everywhere, and $\bm{G}(\bm{0}) = \bm{G}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $(\bm{W}_{\bm{u}},\sigma^2_{\bm{u}})
:= \bracket{\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},\ \sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}}}$ for the
representative of $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$ used in
\eqref{eq:exponential_map}, which is affine in $\bm{u}$, and let
$\bracket{\tilde{\bm{U}}_j(\bm{u}),\ \tilde{b}_j(\bm{u})}
:= \iota_{(\bm{W}_{\bm{u}},\sigma^2_{\bm{u}})}
\bracket{\Gamma^{-1}_{\bm{u}}(\bm{e}_j)}$ be the horizontal lift there.
By \eqref{eq:G_def} and \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional} applied at the point
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$,

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{G}(\bm{u})_{jk} = \frac{1}{2}\tr\curly{
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{N}_j(\bm{u})
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{N}_k(\bm{u})},
  \qquad
  \bm{N}_j(\bm{u}) := \tilde{\bm{U}}_j(\bm{u})\bm{W}_{\bm{u}}^T
  + \bm{W}_{\bm{u}}\tilde{\bm{U}}_j(\bm{u})^T
  + \tilde{b}_j(\bm{u})\bm{I}_p .
\end{equation*}
Each ingredient is smooth in $\bm{u}$: the matrix $\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}$
is polynomial in $\bm{u}$ by \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} and is bounded
below by $c_1\bm{I}_p$ by \eqref{eq:spectral_bounds}, so
$\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}$ is smooth; $\Gamma^{-1}_{\bm{u}}$ is smooth by
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} and
