Definition~\ref{def:transport}; and
$\iota_{(\bm{W}_{\bm{u}},\sigma^2_{\bm{u}})}$ is smooth in $\bm{u}$ by
part 3 of Lemma~\ref{thm:lyapunov_lemma}.
That $\bm{G}(\bm{0}) = \bm{G}$ is $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$, and
positive definiteness is Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} at the point
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$, the change of frame being invertible.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{The influence operator of a locally regular sequence}

\begin{proposition}[Regularity as a normalization of the influence operator]\label{thm:regular_IF}
Let $T_n$ be a localizable estimator sequence which is asymptotically
linear at $\theta_0$ in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:asymptotic_linearity}, with influence operator
$\text{IF}' : \mathbb{R}^p \to T_{\theta_0}M$.
In the coordinates fixed in \eqref{eq:Delta_n_def}, let
$\bm{V} := \text{Cov}\bracket{\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1)}$ and
$\bm{K} := \mathbb{E}\squared{\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1)\,
\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)^T}$ be its covariance matrix and its
cross-covariance matrix with the score.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_third_lemma}
    \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] \rightsquigarrow
    N\bracket{\bm{K}\bm{h},\ \bm{V}}
    \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} .
  \end{equation}
  Consequently $T_n$ is locally regular at $\theta_0$ if and only if
  $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$, that is, if and only if
  \eqref{eq:IF_normalization} holds; and in that case the limit law is
  $L = N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{V}}$.
  \item If $T_n$ is locally regular then $\bm{V} \succeq \bm{G}^{-1}$,
  with equality if and only if
  $\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1) = \text{IF}(\bm{X}_1)$ almost surely,
  $\text{IF}$ being the influence function
  \eqref{eq:influence_function} of $\hat{\theta}_n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.}
Write $\bm{\psi}_i := \text{IF}'(\bm{X}_i) \in \mathbb{R}^d$ and
$\bm{\Psi}_n := n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{\psi}_i$, so that
Definition~\ref{def:asymptotic_linearity} reads
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = \bm{\Psi}_n + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$.
The vectors
$\bracket{\bm{\psi}_i,\ \nabla l(\bm{X}_i;\theta_0)} \in \mathbb{R}^{2d}$
are independent, identically distributed, centred and square integrable,
the second block by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} and the first by
Definition~\ref{def:asymptotic_linearity}, so the multivariate central
limit theorem gives

\begin{equation*}
  \bracket{\bm{\Psi}_n,\ \bm{\Delta}_n} \rightsquigarrow
  N\bracket{\bm{0},\
  \begin{pmatrix} \bm{V} & \bm{K} \\ \bm{K}^T & \bm{G}\end{pmatrix}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0},
\end{equation*}
with $\bm{\Delta}_n$ the normalized score \eqref{eq:Delta_n_def}.
Applying the continuous mapping theorem to the linear map
$(\bm{a},\bm{\delta}) \mapsto \bracket{\bm{a},\ \bm{\delta}^T\bm{h}
-\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}}$, and then Slutsky's lemma together
with the expansion \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} of $\Lambda_n(\bm{h})$,

\begin{equation*}
  \bracket{\bm{\Psi}_n,\ \Lambda_n(\bm{h})} \rightsquigarrow
  N\bracket{
  \begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}\\ -\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}\end{pmatrix},\
  \begin{pmatrix} \bm{V} & \bm{K}\bm{h} \\
  \bm{h}^T\bm{K}^T & \bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}\end{pmatrix}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0},
\end{equation*}
the off-diagonal block being
$\text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{\psi}_1,\ \nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)^T\bm{h}}
= \bm{K}\bm{h}$.
This is again the situation of Example 6.7 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, so Le Cam's third lemma gives
$\bm{\Psi}_n \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{K}\bm{h},\bm{V}}$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$; and
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]-\bm{\Psi}_n = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ as well, by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}.
This proves \eqref{eq:IF_third_lemma}.

In particular $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, so Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} applies and
makes local regularity equivalent to the requirement that
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]-\bm{h}$ have a limit law free of $\bm{h}$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$.
By \eqref{eq:IF_third_lemma} that limit is
$N\bracket{\bracket{\bm{K}-\bm{I}_d}\bm{h},\ \bm{V}}$, whose mean must
therefore not depend on $\bm{h}$; taking $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ shows that the
constant value is $\bm{0}$, so $\bracket{\bm{K}-\bm{I}_d}\bm{h} = \bm{0}$
for every $\bm{h}$ and $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$.
Conversely $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$ makes that limit $N(\bm{0},\bm{V})$ for
every $\bm{h}$.
Finally, for $\bm{z} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ the $j$-th coordinate of
$\mathbb{E}\squared{s(\bm{X};\theta_0)(\bm{z})\,\text{IF}'(\bm{X})}$ is
$\mathbb{E}\squared{\bracket{\nabla l(\bm{X};\theta_0)^T\bm{z}}
\bm{\psi}_{1j}} = \bracket{\bm{K}\bm{z}}_j$, so $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$ and
\eqref{eq:IF_normalization} are the same statement.

\textbf{2.}
Assume $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$ and put
$\bm{D} := \bm{\psi}_1 - \bm{G}^{-1}\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)$, which
is centred and square integrable.
Then $\mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{D}\,\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)^T}
= \bm{K}-\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G} = \bm{0}$, so $\bm{D}$ is uncorrelated with
$\bm{G}^{-1}\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)$ and

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{V} = \text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{G}^{-1}
  \nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)} + \text{Cov}(\bm{D})
  = \bm{G}^{-1}+\text{Cov}(\bm{D}) \succeq \bm{G}^{-1},
\end{equation*}
using $\text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{G}^{-1}\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)}
= \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G}\bm{G}^{-1} = \bm{G}^{-1}$.
Equality forces $\text{Cov}(\bm{D}) = \bm{0}$ and hence, $\bm{D}$ being
centred, $\bm{D} = \bm{0}$ almost surely, which by
\eqref{eq:influence_function} and the identification made at the end of
the proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} says
$\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1) = \text{IF}(\bm{X}_1)$ almost surely.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:regular_IF_comments}
Part 2 is the convolution theorem \eqref{eq:convolution} restricted to the
asymptotically linear class, proved from scratch in five lines rather than
cited: the decomposition
$\bm{\psi}_1 = \bm{G}^{-1}\nabla l(\bm{X}_1;\theta_0)+\bm{D}$ with
$\bm{D}$ uncorrelated with the score is exactly the decomposition
$L = N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}} * \mu$, read at the level of influence
operators rather than of limit laws.
Part 1 should be read alongside Proposition~\ref{thm:IF_unique}: taken
together they say that an asymptotically linear sequence has exactly one
influence operator, that local regularity is the single linear condition
\eqref{eq:IF_normalization} on it, and that among the influence operators
satisfying that condition the score-proportional one
\eqref{eq:influence_function} is the unique minimizer of the asymptotic
covariance.

Note also where local regularity is \textit{not} needed.
Part 1 shows that \eqref{eq:IF_third_lemma} holds for \textit{any}
asymptotically linear sequence, so the behaviour under local alternatives
is always computable, from $\bm{K}$ alone; what regularity adds is not the
computability but the answer $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$.
The Hodges sequence of Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} is the natural
illustration.
At $\theta_0 = \theta_*$ it is localizable, being equal to
$\theta_* \in \mathcal{V}$ with probability tending to one, and on that
same event
$\hat{\bm{u}}_n\squared{\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}}
= \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_*}(\theta_*) = \bm{0}$, so that
$\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n\squared{\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}}
= o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_*}$.
It is therefore asymptotically linear at $\theta_*$ with influence
operator $\text{IF}' \equiv \bm{0}$, which is centred and square
integrable; hence $\bm{K} = \bm{0}$ and $\bm{V} = \bm{0}$, and part 1
predicts
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n\squared{\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}}
\rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{K}\bm{h},\bm{V}}
= N(\bm{0},\bm{0}) = \delta_{\bm{0}}$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ for every $\bm{h}$, which is exactly
what Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} computes by hand.
It violates \eqref{eq:IF_normalization} as flagrantly as possible,
$\bm{K} = \bm{0}$ being as far from $\bm{I}_d$ as a matrix can be.
So the Hodges sequence is no counterexample to
\eqref{eq:IF_normalization}, which it simply violates while failing to be
locally regular; it is a counterexample to dispensing with the hypothesis
of local regularity in \eqref{eq:convolution}.
Part 2 is untouched by it, since part 2 assumes $\bm{K} = \bm{I}_d$ and
it is precisely that assumption which fails here; no contradiction arises
from $\bm{V} = \bm{0} \prec \bm{G}^{-1}$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Asymptotic normality as a random point of the manifold}

Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} and Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}
are statements about a random \textit{vector},
$\sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\hat{\theta}_n}$, living in
the fixed vector space $T_{\theta_0}M$.
It is natural to ask what they say about the random \textit{point}
$\hat{\theta}_n \in M$ itself, in the spirit of Example III.1 of
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, where a Gaussian law on a manifold is a law
whose density is proportional to
$\exp\bracket{-\frac{d(x,\mu)^2}{2\tau^2}}$ and is centred at a point
$\mu$ of the manifold.
The obstruction to asking the question naively is that $M$ carries no
scalar multiplication, so the rescaling by $\sqrt{n}$ that all these
statements involve cannot be performed on $M$; and the dilation
$\theta \mapsto \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,
\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\theta)}$, which one might substitute for it,
does not map $M$ into itself, the domain $B_{\rho}$ of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} being a bounded ball.
The rescaling must therefore be moved onto the \textit{metric}: one asks
not that the law of $\hat{\theta}_n$ converge --- it converges to
$\delta_{\theta_0}$, which says only that $\hat{\theta}_n$ is consistent
--- but that it be indistinguishable, at the resolution
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$, from an explicit Gaussian-shaped law on $M$ centred
at the true parameter.
That is the formulation below, and it turns out to be exactly local
regularity.

The formulation uses the Riemannian distance of $(M,g)$, so we first
record that this distance is finite --- a fact needed nowhere earlier and
therefore not yet stated.

\begin{lemma}[$M$ is connected]\label{thm:M_connected}
The manifold $M$ is connected; consequently its Riemannian distance
$d_M$ is finite on $M \times M$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $\text{St} := \curly{\bm{U} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q} :
\bm{U}^T\bm{U} = \bm{I}_q}$ and let $\text{Sym}^{++}(q)$ be the set of
positive definite $q \times q$ symmetric matrices, which is convex and
hence connected.
The continuous map $(\bm{U},\bm{P}) \mapsto \bm{U}\bm{P}$ from
$\text{St} \times \text{Sym}^{++}(q)$ into $\mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ has
as image exactly the set of rank $q$ matrices: any such $\bm{A}$ is
$\bm{U}\bm{P}$ with $\bm{P} := \bracket{\bm{A}^T\bm{A}}^{\frac{1}{2}}$,
which is positive definite, and $\bm{U} := \bm{A}\bm{P}^{-1}$, which
satisfies $\bm{U}^T\bm{U} = \bm{P}^{-1}\bm{A}^T\bm{A}\bm{P}^{-1}
= \bm{I}_q$.
The set $\text{St}$ is itself connected, being the image of the connected
group $SO(p)$ under $\bm{R} \mapsto \bm{R}\bm{E}$, where
$\bm{E} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ consists of the first $q$ columns of
$\bm{I}_p$: given $\bm{U} \in \text{St}$, complete it to
$\squared{\bm{U}\ \ \bm{U}^{\perp}} \in O(p)$ and, if the determinant is
$-1$, change the sign of one column of $\bm{U}^{\perp}$, which is
possible precisely because $q < p$.
So the set of rank $q$ matrices is connected, hence so is $\Theta$, the
product of that set with $(0,\infty)$, and hence so is its continuous
image $M = \pi(\Theta)$.
A connected Riemannian manifold is path connected, and the infimum of the
lengths of the joining curves is finite, so $d_M < \infty$ throughout.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Wrapped normal laws and the rescaled metric]\label{def:wrapped_normal}
Let $d_M$ denote the Riemannian distance of the connected Riemannian
manifold $(M,g)$, and write
$\text{law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y)$ for the law of a random element $Y$ under
$\mathbb{P}$, the subscript being omitted when the law is clear.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For $\theta \in M$, a centred Gaussian law $\nu$ on $T_{\theta}M$
  and $n \geqslant 1$, let $\nu^{(n)}$ be the law of
  $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z}$ with $\bm{Z} \sim \nu$, conditioned on the
  event $\curly{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z} \in \mathcal{E}_{\theta}}$, which
  has positive probability because $\mathcal{E}_{\theta}$ is an open ball
  about the origin; and put

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:wrapped_def}
    \mathcal{N}_n(\theta,\nu)
    := \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta}}_{\#}\,\nu^{(n)},
  \end{equation}
  the push-forward of $\nu^{(n)}$ by the exponential map at $\theta$: the
  \textit{wrapped normal law} on $M$ with centre $\theta$, dispersion
  $n^{-1}$ and shape $\nu$.
  We abbreviate $\mathcal{N}_n(\theta) := \mathcal{N}_n\bracket{\theta,\
  N\bracket{\bm{0},G_{\theta}^{-1}}}$, the shape being given by the
  inverse Fisher information operator at $\theta$ itself.
  \item For Borel probability measures $P,Q$ on $M$ put

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:BL_n_def}
    \text{BL}_n(P,Q) := \sup\curly{
    \abs{\int_M f\,\mathrm{d}P - \int_M f\,\mathrm{d}Q}\ : \
    \|f\|_{\infty} \leqslant 1, \
    \abs{f(x)-f(y)} \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,d_M(x,y)
    \ \ \forall\, x,y \in M},
  \end{equation}
  the bounded Lipschitz distance for the rescaled metric
  $\sqrt{n}\,d_M$, a genuine metric by Lemma~\ref{thm:M_connected}; and
  write $\text{BL}$ for the ordinary bounded Lipschitz distance on
  $T_{\theta_0}M \cong \mathbb{R}^d$, which metrizes weak convergence
  there.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

Two comments on the definition.
First, the conditioning in part 1 is a formality for every $\theta$ in
the compact set
$\mathcal{K} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\overline{B_{r/2}}}
\subseteq \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_r)$, and this is all we shall need,
since the centres occurring below are $\theta_0$ and $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$,
for which $\abs{\bm{h}}/\sqrt{n} \leqslant \frac{r}{2}$ once $n$ is
large.
For $\theta = [\bm{A},s]$ ranging in $\mathcal{K}$ the radius
$\min\bracket{\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}),s}$ of $\mathcal{E}_{\theta}$ is a
positive continuous function of $\theta$, as observed in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}, hence is bounded below by some
$\rho' > 0$; and the operator norm of $G^{-1}_{\theta}$ equals that of
$\bm{G}\bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\theta)}^{-1}$, the frame of
\eqref{eq:G_transported} being orthonormal, which is bounded above on
$\overline{B_{r/2}}$ by Lemma~\ref{thm:information_smooth}.
The Gaussian tail bound then gives
$\mathbb{P}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z} \notin \mathcal{E}_{\theta}}
\leqslant \mathbb{P}\bracket{\abs{\bm{Z}} > \sqrt{n}\rho'}
= O\bracket{e^{-cn}}$ for some $c > 0$, uniformly over
$\theta \in \mathcal{K}$, so the conditioning perturbs the law by
$O\bracket{e^{-cn}}$ in total variation and is invisible in everything
that follows.
The same bound holds, a fortiori, whenever the shape $\nu$ has a
covariance whose operator norm is bounded along the sequence, which is
the only property of it used below.
Second, $\text{BL}_n$ is nondecreasing in $n$, the class of competing
$f$ growing with $n$, and $\text{BL}_n(P,Q) \to 0$ is a strictly stronger
requirement than weak convergence: it says that $P$ and $Q$ cannot be
told apart by any bounded test function varying on the scale
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$, which is the scale on which $\hat{\theta}_n$ and
$\theta_0$ differ.
Two laws both collapsing to $\delta_{\theta_0}$ are automatically close in
the weak topology and the closeness says nothing; $\text{BL}_n$ is what
removes that triviality.

\begin{lemma}[The normal chart is bi-Lipschitz]\label{thm:bilipschitz}
There exist $\varepsilon \in (0,r]$ and $L \geqslant 1$, depending only on
$\theta_0$, such that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:bilipschitz}
  L^{-1}\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}
  \ \leqslant \ d_M\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u}),\
  \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}
  \ \leqslant \ L\,\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_{\varepsilon} .
\end{equation}
Moreover $d_M\bracket{\theta_0,\ \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}
= \abs{\bm{u}}$ for every $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\varepsilon_0 \in (0,r]$.
By part 2 of Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} the map
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ is a diffeomorphism of $B_{\rho}$ onto the open
set $\mathcal{U}$, so $\mathrm{d}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}}_{\bm{u}}$
is a linear isomorphism for every $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$; by continuity and
compactness of $\overline{B_{\varepsilon_0}} \subseteq B_{\rho}$ there is
$L_0 \geqslant 1$ with

\begin{equation}\label{eq:metric_comparable}
  L_0^{-1}\abs{\bm{\xi}}
  \leqslant \abs{\mathrm{d}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}}_{\bm{u}}
  \bracket{\bm{\xi}}}_g
  \leqslant L_0\abs{\bm{\xi}}
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{u} \in \overline{B_{\varepsilon_0}}
  \text{ and } \bm{\xi} \in T_{\theta_0}M,
\end{equation}
where $\abs{\, \cdot \,}_g$ is the norm of $g$ at the relevant point.

The upper bound in \eqref{eq:bilipschitz} follows with $L = L_0$ for all
$\bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_{\varepsilon_0}$: the segment
$t \mapsto \bm{u}+t(\bm{w}-\bm{u})$ stays in the convex set
$B_{\varepsilon_0}$, its image under $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ joins the two
points and has, by \eqref{eq:metric_comparable}, $g$-length at most
$L_0\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}$, and $d_M$ is the infimum of the lengths of such
curves.

For the lower bound put $\varepsilon := \frac{\varepsilon_0}{4}$ and take
$\bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_{\varepsilon}$, so that
$\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}} < \frac{\varepsilon_0}{2}$.
Let $c$ be any piecewise smooth curve in $M$ from
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$ to $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})$.
If $c$ stays inside
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{B_{\frac{\varepsilon_0}{2}}}$, then
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0} \circ \, c$ is a curve in
$B_{\frac{\varepsilon_0}{2}}$ from $\bm{u}$ to $\bm{w}$, of Euclidean
length at least $\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}$, so \eqref{eq:metric_comparable}
gives $\text{length}(c) \geqslant L_0^{-1}\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}$.
Otherwise, let $t_*$ be the first time at which $c$ leaves
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{B_{\frac{\varepsilon_0}{2}}}$; the
restriction of $\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0} \circ \, c$ to $[0,t_*]$ joins
$\bm{u}$, of norm less than $\frac{\varepsilon_0}{4}$, to a point of norm
$\frac{\varepsilon_0}{2}$, hence has Euclidean length at least
$\frac{\varepsilon_0}{4} > \frac{1}{2}\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}$, and
$\text{length}(c) \geqslant \frac{1}{2}L_0^{-1}\abs{\bm{u}-\bm{w}}$.
Taking the infimum over $c$ and setting $L := 2L_0$ gives
\eqref{eq:bilipschitz}.

The last assertion is the Gauss lemma.
Since $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ is a diffeomorphism of the ball
$B_{\rho} \subseteq T_{\theta_0}M$ onto $\mathcal{U}$, the set
$\mathcal{U}$ is a geodesic ball centred at $\theta_0$, and inside a
geodesic ball the radial geodesic
$t \mapsto \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(t\bm{u})$, $t \in [0,1]$, is the unique
minimizing curve in $M$ from $\theta_0$ to $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$
(\citealp{Lee_2018}, Chapter 6).
Its length is $\abs{\bm{u}}$, geodesics having constant speed and the
initial speed being $\abs{\bm{u}}$.
\end{proof}

We fix $\varepsilon$ and $L$ as in Lemma~\ref{thm:bilipschitz} for the
rest of this subsection and write
$\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_{\varepsilon})
\subseteq \mathcal{V}$.
The next lemma is the translation device: it says that comparing two
$M$-valued sequences in $\text{BL}_n$ is the same as comparing their
rescaled chart images in the ordinary bounded Lipschitz distance, so that
every statement about random points of $M$ made below reduces to a
statement about random vectors of $T_{\theta_0}M$, where the preceding
results live.

\begin{lemma}[Rescaled comparison]\label{thm:BL_transfer}
For each $n$ let $Y_n$ and $Y_n'$ be $M$-valued random elements, possibly
defined under different laws, with
$\mathbb{P}\bracket{Y_n \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}} \to 1$ and
$\mathbb{P}\bracket{Y_n' \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}} \to 1$, and put

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{y}_n := \begin{cases}
    \sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(Y_n)
      & \text{on } \curly{Y_n \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}},\\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
and likewise $\bm{y}_n'$ from $Y_n'$.
Then, with $P_n := \text{law}(Y_n)$ and $P_n' := \text{law}(Y_n')$ on $M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:BL_equivalence}
  \text{BL}_n\bracket{P_n,P_n'} \longrightarrow 0
  \qquad \Longleftrightarrow \qquad
  \text{BL}\bracket{\text{law}(\bm{y}_n),\ \text{law}(\bm{y}_n')}
  \longrightarrow 0 .
\end{equation}
In particular, if $\bm{y}_n$ and $\bm{y}_n'$ converge in distribution to
the same limit, then $\text{BL}_n\bracket{P_n,P_n'} \to 0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Abbreviate
$\eta_n := \mathbb{P}\bracket{Y_n \notin \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}}
+ \mathbb{P}\bracket{Y_n' \notin \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}} \to 0$.

$(\Leftarrow)$
Let $f : M \to \mathbb{R}$ satisfy $\|f\|_{\infty} \leqslant 1$ and
$\abs{f(x)-f(y)} \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,d_M(x,y)$, and define
$g$ on $\sqrt{n}B_{\varepsilon}$ by
$g(\bm{z}) := f\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{z}}}$.
By the upper bound in \eqref{eq:bilipschitz},
$\abs{g(\bm{z})-g(\bm{z}')} \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,L\,n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\abs{\bm{z}-\bm{z}'} = L\abs{\bm{z}-\bm{z}'}$, and
$\|g\|_{\infty} \leqslant 1$.
Extend $g$ to all of $T_{\theta_0}M$ by the McShane formula
$\tilde{g}(\bm{z}) := \inf_{\bm{z}' \in \sqrt{n}B_{\varepsilon}}
\bracket{g(\bm{z}')+L\abs{\bm{z}-\bm{z}'}}$, truncated to $[-1,1]$; both
bounds are preserved and $\tilde{g} = g$ on $\sqrt{n}B_{\varepsilon}$.
On $\curly{Y_n \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}}$ we have
$f(Y_n) = \tilde{g}(\bm{y}_n)$, and both are bounded by $1$ everywhere,
so

\begin{equation*}
  \abs{\int_M f\,\mathrm{d}P_n - \int_M f\,\mathrm{d}P_n'}
  \leqslant \abs{\mathbb{E}\squared{\tilde{g}(\bm{y}_n)}
  - \mathbb{E}\squared{\tilde{g}(\bm{y}_n')}} + 2\eta_n
  \leqslant L\,\text{BL}\bracket{\text{law}(\bm{y}_n),\
  \text{law}(\bm{y}_n')} + 2\eta_n,
\end{equation*}
the last step because $L^{-1}\tilde{g}$ is bounded by $1$ and
$1$-Lipschitz, $L$ being at least $1$.
Taking the supremum over $f$ gives
$\text{BL}_n(P_n,P_n') \leqslant L\,\text{BL} + 2\eta_n$.

$(\Rightarrow)$
Symmetrically, let $g : T_{\theta_0}M \to \mathbb{R}$ satisfy
$\|g\|_{\infty} \leqslant 1$ and $\text{Lip}(g) \leqslant 1$, and define
$f$ on $\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}$ by
$f(x) := g\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(x)}$.
For $x,y \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}$ the lower bound in
\eqref{eq:bilipschitz} gives
$\abs{f(x)-f(y)} \leqslant \sqrt{n}\abs{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(x)
-\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(y)}
\leqslant L\,\sqrt{n}\,d_M(x,y)$, so $f$ is $L$-Lipschitz for the metric
$\sqrt{n}d_M$ on $\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}$; extend it to $M$ by the
McShane formula for that metric --- finite by
Lemma~\ref{thm:M_connected}, which is what makes the formula meaningful
--- again truncated to $[-1,1]$.
The same two displays, read in the other direction, give
$\text{BL}\bracket{\text{law}(\bm{y}_n),\text{law}(\bm{y}_n')}
\leqslant L\,\text{BL}_n(P_n,P_n')+2\eta_n$.

The final assertion holds because $\text{BL}$ metrizes weak convergence on
$T_{\theta_0}M \cong \mathbb{R}^d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The maximum likelihood estimator as an asymptotically wrapped normal point of $M$]\label{thm:manifold_normality}
Let $1 \leqslant q < p$ and $\theta_0 = \squared{\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2}
\in M$, and let $\mathcal{N}_n(\, \cdot \,)$ and $\text{BL}_n$ be as in
Definition~\ref{def:wrapped_normal}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textit{(Fixed centre.)}
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:manifold_normality_null}
    \text{BL}_n\bracket{\text{law}_{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}}
    \bracket{\hat{\theta}_n},\ \mathcal{N}_n(\theta_0)}
    \longrightarrow 0 .
  \end{equation}
  That is, at the resolution $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ the maximum likelihood
  estimator is a wrapped normal random point of $M$, centred at
  $\squared{\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2}$, with dispersion $n^{-1}$ and with
  shape the inverse Fisher information operator.
  \item \textit{(Moving centre.)}
  For every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:manifold_normality_alt}
    \text{BL}_n\bracket{\text{law}_{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}
    \bracket{\hat{\theta}_n},\
    \mathcal{N}_n\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}} \longrightarrow 0,
  \end{equation}
  the approximating law being the wrapped normal centred at the true
  parameter $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$, with shape the inverse Fisher
  information operator at that same point.
  \item \textit{(The moving centre statement is local regularity.)}
  Let $T_n$ be a localizable estimator sequence with
  $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
  $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, let $\nu$ be a centred Gaussian law on
  $T_{\theta_0}M$, and for $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$ let $\nu_{\bm{u}}$
  denote its transport to $T_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}M$ by the
  linear isometry $\Gamma_{\bm{u}}^{-1}$ of
  Definition~\ref{def:transport}.
  Then $T_n$ is locally regular at $\theta_0$ with limit law $\nu$, in
  the sense of Definition~\ref{def:regular}, if and only if
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:manifold_normality_iff}
    \text{BL}_n\bracket{\text{law}_{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}
    \bracket{T_n},\ \mathcal{N}_n\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}},\
    \nu_{\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}}}} \longrightarrow 0
    \qquad \text{for every } \bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M .
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ and abbreviate
$\bm{u}_n := \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}$ and
$\theta_n := \theta_{n,\bm{h}} = \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u}_n)$.
Parts 1 and 2 are proved together, part 1 being the case
$\bm{h} = \bm{0}$, in which $\bm{u}_n = \bm{0}$, $\theta_n = \theta_0$ and
$\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$; part 3 is then read off the same
computation.
Throughout, $\bm{G}(\, \cdot \,)$ is the transported information matrix
\eqref{eq:G_transported} of Lemma~\ref{thm:information_smooth}.

\textbf{Step 1: the estimator side.}
By Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} and
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity},
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_n}\bracket{\hat{\theta}_n \in
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}} \to 1$, and \eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion}
gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MN_estimator_side}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n \rightsquigarrow
  N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_n} .
\end{equation}
For $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ this is Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}
itself, and neither Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} nor
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} is used.

\textbf{Step 2: the wrapped normal side.}
Let $\Theta_n \sim \mathcal{N}_n\bracket{\theta_n,\lambda_n}$, where
$\lambda_n := N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_n}}$ for parts 1 and 2 and
$\lambda_n := \nu_{\bm{u}_n}$ for part 3.
In both cases $\lambda_n$ is a centred Gaussian law on $T_{\theta_n}M$,
and we compute its covariance matrix in the frame
$\Gamma^{-1}_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{e}_1),\ldots,\Gamma^{-1}_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{e}_d)$
of $T_{\theta_n}M$, which is $g_{\theta_n}$-orthonormal because
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ is $g_{\theta_0}$-orthonormal and
$\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}$ is a linear isometry.
The coordinates of a vector $\bm{v} \in T_{\theta_n}M$ in that frame are
by construction the $\bm{e}$-coordinates of
$\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{v}) \in T_{\theta_0}M$.
For parts 1 and 2 the matrix of the bilinear form $G_{\theta_n}$ in that
frame is $\bm{G}(\bm{u}_n)$, by the very definition
\eqref{eq:G_transported}, so the covariance matrix of $\lambda_n$ is
$\bm{G}(\bm{u}_n)^{-1}$; for part 3 it is the fixed covariance matrix
$\bm{C}_{\nu}$ of $\nu$ in the basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$,
transport being an isometry.
Either way the covariance stays bounded, so the conditioning built into
$\lambda_n^{(n)}$ costs $O\bracket{e^{-cn}}$ in total variation, as
recorded after Definition~\ref{def:wrapped_normal}, and may be discarded.
Writing $\Theta_n = \text{Exp}_{\theta_n}(\bm{v}_n)$ with
$\bm{v}_n \sim \lambda_n^{(n)}$, the vector
$\sqrt{n}\,\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{v}_n)$ therefore has, in the basis
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$, the law $N\bracket{\bm{0},
\bm{G}(\bm{u}_n)^{-1}}$, respectively $N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{C}_{\nu}}$;
in particular
$\sqrt{n}\abs{\bm{v}_n} = \sqrt{n}\abs{\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{v}_n)}
= O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.

We check next that $\Theta_n \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}$ with
probability tending to one.
The set of pairs $(x,\bm{v})$ in $TM$ with $\bm{v} \in \mathcal{E}_x$ and
$\text{Exp}_x(\bm{v}) \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}$ is open, the domain
of $\text{Exp}$ being open in $TM$ and $\text{Exp}$ continuous on it as
established in the proof of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}, and it contains
$(\theta_0,\bm{0})$; hence there is $\delta > 0$ such that
$\text{Exp}_x(\bm{v}) \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}$ whenever
$x \in \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_{\delta})$ and $\abs{\bm{v}} < \delta$.
Since $\abs{\bm{u}_n} \to 0$ and
$\mathbb{P}\bracket{\abs{\bm{v}_n} < \delta} \to 1$, the claim follows.
Put $\bm{w}_n := \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\Theta_n)$ on that event and
$\bm{w}_n := \bm{0}$ off it.

On that event $\bm{u}_n$ and $\bm{w}_n$ lie in $B_r$, so $\theta_n$ and
$\Theta_n$ lie in $\mathcal{V}^{\sharp}$; since moreover
$\bm{v}_n \in \mathcal{E}_{\theta_n}$ and
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_n}(\bm{v}_n) = \Theta_n$, the uniqueness clause of
part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart} identifies $\bm{v}_n$ with
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_n}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w}_n)}$,
and the definition \eqref{eq:F_def} of $F$ then reads
$\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{v}_n) = F\bracket{\bm{u}_n,\bm{w}_n}$.
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MN_wrapped_expansion}
  \abs{F\bracket{\bm{u}_n,\bm{w}_n}-\bracket{\bm{w}_n-\bm{u}_n}}
  \leqslant C\abs{\bm{u}_n}\abs{\bm{w}_n-\bm{u}_n},
\end{equation}
whence first, by the triangle inequality and because
$\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}$ is an isometry,
$\abs{\bm{w}_n-\bm{u}_n} \leqslant
\frac{\abs{\bm{v}_n}}{1-C\abs{\bm{u}_n}}$ for $n$ large enough that
$C\abs{\bm{u}_n} < 1$, so that
$\sqrt{n}\abs{\bm{w}_n-\bm{u}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$; and then, feeding
this back into \eqref{eq:MN_wrapped_expansion} multiplied by $\sqrt{n}$,

\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{n}\,\bm{w}_n
  = \bm{h} + \sqrt{n}\,\Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}(\bm{v}_n)
  + O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}} .
\end{equation*}
By Lemma~\ref{thm:information_smooth},
$\bm{G}(\bm{u}_n) \to \bm{G}(\bm{0}) = \bm{G}$, so
$N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}(\bm{u}_n)^{-1}}
\rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}}$; Slutsky's lemma
therefore gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MN_wrapped_side}
  \sqrt{n}\,\bm{w}_n \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}}
  \quad \text{in parts 1 and 2},
  \qquad
  \sqrt{n}\,\bm{w}_n \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{C}_{\nu}}
  \quad \text{in part 3}.
\end{equation}

\textbf{Step 3: conclusion.}
Parts 1 and 2 follow from Lemma~\ref{thm:BL_transfer} applied with
$Y_n := \hat{\theta}_n$ under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_n}$ and
$Y_n' := \Theta_n$: the localization hypotheses were verified in Steps 1
and 2, the chart error of Lemma~\ref{thm:BL_transfer} agrees with
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ on
$\curly{\hat{\theta}_n \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}}$, an event of
probability tending to one, and the two rescaled chart images have the
same weak limit $N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}}$ by
\eqref{eq:MN_estimator_side} and \eqref{eq:MN_wrapped_side}.

For part 3, apply the equivalence \eqref{eq:BL_equivalence} of the same
lemma with $Y_n := T_n$ under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_n}$ and
$Y_n' := \Theta_n$; the hypothesis
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ together with
localizability and Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} gives
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_n}\bracket{T_n \in \mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}} \to 1$
and makes the two versions of the chart error agree there.
The left hand side of \eqref{eq:BL_equivalence} is then
\eqref{eq:manifold_normality_iff} for the given $\bm{h}$, and by
\eqref{eq:MN_wrapped_side} and the fact that $\text{BL}$ metrizes weak
convergence its right hand side holds if and only if
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] \rightsquigarrow
N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{C}_{\nu}}$ under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_n}$, that is,
if and only if $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]-\bm{h} \rightsquigarrow \nu$
there.
Requiring this for every $\bm{h}$ is exactly condition
\eqref{eq:chart_regularity} of Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity}, which
that lemma shows to be equivalent to local regularity with limit law
$\nu$; and its last assertion makes the boundedness hypothesis automatic
on the regularity side.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Reading the theorem]\label{rmk:manifold_normality}
\textbf{1. What each part costs.}
Part 1 uses Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} and the chart estimate
Lemma~\ref{thm:bilipschitz}, and nothing else; in particular it does
\textit{not} use local regularity.
It does not really use the transport comparison
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} either: at $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ one has
$\bm{u}_n = \bm{0}$, $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$ and therefore
$\bm{w}_n = \bm{v}_n$ exactly, so that
\eqref{eq:MN_wrapped_expansion} is invoked only in the trivial slice
$R(\bm{0},\, \cdot \,) = \bm{0}$ already recorded in the proof of that
lemma.
Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence} below is different in this respect: it
needs the transport comparison even at $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$, because there
the moving base point is $\hat{\theta}_n$ and not $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$.
Part 2 is part 1 with the centre moved, and the only ingredient that
changes is \eqref{eq:MN_estimator_side}, which for $\bm{h} \neq \bm{0}$ is
precisely \eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion}, the substance of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}.
Part 3 says that this is not an artefact of the proof: for a localizable
sequence with the boundedness property, the moving centre statement and
local regularity are the \textit{same} statement.
So the answer to the question whether local regularity is needed for
asymptotic normality at the level of the manifold is: not for the version
centred at a fixed, known $\theta_0$, which is a restatement of
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}; and for the version centred at the
true parameter, whatever it is, local regularity is not merely sufficient
but necessary.

\textbf{2. The shape may be perturbed.}
Step 2 uses about the shape only that its covariance, read in the
transported frame, is bounded and converges to $\bm{G}^{-1}$.
Part 2 therefore also holds with $\mathcal{N}_n\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$
replaced by
$\mathcal{N}_n\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}},\
\bracket{N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0}}}_{\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}}}$, the
transport of the information at $\theta_0$ itself: at the resolution
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ the shape need not be re-evaluated at the moving
centre.
What may \textit{not} be moved is the centre, which is the content of the
next comment.

\textbf{3. Why the moving centre version is the one that means
something.}
The centre of the approximating law in
\eqref{eq:manifold_normality_null} is a point the statistician must be
told.
The centre in \eqref{eq:manifold_normality_alt} is the true parameter,
whichever member of the contiguous family it is.
By Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} no procedure can distinguish these members,
so only the second version supports the sentence one actually wants to
say, namely that $\hat{\theta}_n$ is distributed around the truth like a
wrapped normal law with dispersion $n^{-1}$ and shape $G^{-1}$; and it
is that sentence which Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence} below converts into
a confidence region.
The Hodges-type sequence of Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} satisfies the
analogue of part 1 at every $\theta_0 \neq \theta_*$, and at $\theta_*$
satisfies a degenerate analogue with $\mathcal{N}_n$ replaced by
$\delta_{\theta_*}$; it satisfies no version of part 2 at $\theta_*$,
since by \eqref{eq:chart_form} its transported error converges to
$\delta_{-\bm{h}}$, which depends on $\bm{h}$.
Part 3 is the formal statement that this is the only way things can go
wrong.

\textbf{4. The link with Example III.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}.}
On $\text{Exp}_{\theta}\bracket{\mathcal{E}_{\theta}}$ the wrapped law
$\mathcal{N}_n(\theta)$ has a density with respect to the Riemannian
volume measure of $(M,g)$ proportional to

\begin{equation}\label{eq:wrapped_density}
  \exp\bracket{-\frac{n}{2}\,G_{\theta}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta}(x),\ \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta}(x)}}
  \cdot J_{\theta}(x)^{-1},
  \qquad
  J_{\theta}(x) := \abs{\det \mathrm{d}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta}}_{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta}(x)}},
\end{equation}
the Jacobian factor converting the Lebesgue measure of the tangent space
into the volume measure.
Two observations.
First, the exponent is the Riemannian Gaussian exponent of
Example III.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, with the quadratic form
$G_{\theta}$ in place of $\tau^{-2}g_{\theta}$: if the information
operator happened to be isotropic, $G_{\theta} = \tau^{-2}g_{\theta}$,
then by the last assertion of Lemma~\ref{thm:bilipschitz} the exponent
would be $-\frac{n}{2\tau^2}\,d_M(\theta,x)^2$, and
\eqref{eq:wrapped_density} would be, up to $J_{\theta}$, the density
displayed in that example with variance parameter $\frac{\tau^2}{n}$.
The Fisher information of the probabilistic principal component analysis
model is not isotropic --- Section~\ref{sec:fisher_explicit} computes it,
and \eqref{eq:D_0_image} already shows that different directions of
$T_{\theta_0}M$ are treated very differently --- so the honest limiting
shape is the anisotropic wrapped law rather than the isotropic Riemannian
Gaussian.
Second, the Jacobian is invisible at the relevant scale: on the support of
$\mathcal{N}_n(\theta)$ one has
$\abs{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta}(x)}
= O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$, and the standard expansion of
the volume density in normal coordinates gives
$J_{\theta}(x) = 1+O\bracket{\abs{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta}(x)}^2}
= 1+O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-1}}$.
At the resolution $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ at which $\text{BL}_n$ measures, the
distinction between the wrapped normal law, the intrinsic Riemannian
Gaussian density and the Gaussian read in the normal chart is therefore
immaterial, and the theorem is insensitive to which of the three is
chosen; we have chosen the wrapped law because it requires no curvature
computation.
Nothing in this observation is used anywhere below; it is recorded only to
say what \eqref{eq:manifold_normality_alt} amounts to.

\textbf{5. What is not claimed.}
The metric $\text{BL}_n$ is a weak metric, and nothing above asserts
convergence in total variation, nor any control of densities; that would
be a genuinely stronger statement, and none of the consequences drawn
below needs it.
Nor is any uniformity in $\bm{h}$ asserted: the statements hold along each
fixed sequence of local alternatives, which is the exact strength of
Definition~\ref{def:regular}, and the genuinely uniform version would
require the local asymptotic normality expansion along arbitrary bounded
sequences $\bm{h}_n$, which Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} as stated does not
provide.
Remark~\ref{rmk:coverage_uniform} says the same thing where it matters.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Locally uniform confidence sets}

The first consequence proper is the inferential payoff, and it is the one
that distinguishes local regularity from
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} in practice.

\begin{corollary}[Locally uniform Wald confidence sets]\label{thm:confidence}
Let $\alpha \in (0,1)$, let $\chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}$ be the $(1-\alpha)$
quantile of the chi-squared law with $d$ degrees of freedom, and define
the random subset of $M$

\begin{equation}\label{eq:confidence_set}
  \mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha} := \text{Exp}_{\hat{\theta}_n}
  \bracket{\curly{\bm{y} \in T_{\hat{\theta}_n}M \ : \
  n\,G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{y},\bm{y}) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}}},
\end{equation}
which is well defined on an event of probability tending to one.
Then, for every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:coverage}
  \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
  \bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}} \in \mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}}
  \longrightarrow 1-\alpha .
\end{equation}
In particular the coverage is asymptotically correct not only at
$\theta_0$, which is the case $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ and needs no more
probabilistic input than Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}, but along
every sequence of local alternatives at the estimation scale
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\bm{h}$ and work under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, which by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} is contiguous to $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$; all
the $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ and $O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ statements of
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} and
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}
therefore remain valid.
In particular $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$,
$\hat{\bm{u}}_n \to \bm{0}$ in probability and
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.

\textit{Well definedness.}
By Lemma~\ref{thm:information_smooth} and the continuous mapping theorem,
$\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) \to \bm{G}$ in probability, so that
$\lambda_{\min}\bracket{\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)}$ is bounded away from zero
in probability.
For any $\bm{y} \in T_{\hat{\theta}_n}M$, the vector
$\bm{z} := \Gamma_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}(\bm{y})$ has the same norm as $\bm{y}$,
$\Gamma_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}$ being a linear isometry, and its coordinates in
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ are the coordinates of $\bm{y}$ in the frame
transported along the radial geodesic; hence, by
\eqref{eq:G_transported},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_lower_bound}
  G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{y},\bm{y})
  = \bm{z}^T\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)\bm{z}
  \geqslant \lambda_{\min}\bracket{\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)}\abs{\bm{y}}^2 .
\end{equation}
The set inside \eqref{eq:confidence_set} is therefore contained in a ball
of radius $O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$ about $\bm{0}$.
Meanwhile the radius of the domain $\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$ of
$\text{Exp}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$, namely
$\min\bracket{\sigma_{\min}(\hat{\bm{W}}_n),\hat{\sigma}_n^2}$, is a
continuous function of the point $\hat{\theta}_n \in M$, as observed in
the proof of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}, and therefore converges in
probability to $\rho > 0$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:consistency}.
Hence, with probability tending to one, that set is a subset of
$\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$ and $\mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}$ is defined.

\textit{Reduction to a quadratic form.}
Both $\hat{\theta}_n$ and $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ lie in $\mathcal{V}$ with
probability tending to one, so part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}
makes $\bm{y}_n := \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\theta_{n,\bm{h}})$
well defined there, and the injectivity of $\text{Exp}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$
on $\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$ shows that, on an event of probability
tending to one, membership $\theta_{n,\bm{h}} \in \mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}$
is equivalent to

\begin{equation}\label{eq:coverage_equivalent}
  n\,G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{y}_n,\bm{y}_n) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha} .
\end{equation}
Put $\bm{z}_n := \Gamma_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}(\bm{y}_n) \in T_{\theta_0}M$, so
that the first equality of \eqref{eq:G_lower_bound}, applied with
$\bm{y} = \bm{y}_n$, reads
$G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{y}_n,\bm{y}_n)
= \bm{z}_n^T\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)\bm{z}_n$.

\textit{The limit.}
Apply Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} with $\bm{u} = \hat{\bm{u}}_n$
and $\bm{w} = \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}$, both in $B_r$ with probability
tending to one, noting that
$F\bracket{\hat{\bm{u}}_n,\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}} = \bm{z}_n$ by
\eqref{eq:F_def}:

\begin{equation*}
  \abs{\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n - \bracket{\bm{h}-\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}}
  \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,C\,\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\,
  \abs{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}-\hat{\bm{u}}_n}
  = \frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\,\abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\,
  \abs{\bm{h}-\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}
  = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}},
\end{equation*}
so that
$\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n = -\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n-\bm{h}}
+ o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.
By \eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion},
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n-\bm{h} \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, and this law is symmetric, so
$\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$.
Combining with $\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) \to \bm{G}$ in probability,
Slutsky's lemma and the continuous mapping theorem applied to
$(\bm{z},\bm{A}) \mapsto \bm{z}^T\bm{A}\bm{z}$ give, through the first
equality of \eqref{eq:G_lower_bound},

\begin{equation*}
  n\,G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{y}_n,\bm{y}_n)
  = \bracket{\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n}^T\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)
  \bracket{\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n}
  \rightsquigarrow \bm{Z}^T\bm{G}\bm{Z},
  \qquad \bm{Z} \sim N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}},
\end{equation*}
and $\bm{Z}^T\bm{G}\bm{Z}
= \|\bm{G}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z}\|^2 \sim \chi^2_d$ because
$\bm{G}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z} \sim N(\bm{0},\bm{I}_d)$.
The distribution function of $\chi^2_d$ being continuous, the probability
of \eqref{eq:coverage_equivalent} converges to
$\mathbb{P}\bracket{\chi^2_d \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}} = 1-\alpha$,
which is \eqref{eq:coverage}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:coverage_uniform}
Three comments.

\textbf{1. The region is a statistic.}
Nothing in \eqref{eq:confidence_set} refers to $\theta_0$: the
exponential map and the information operator are evaluated at
$\hat{\theta}_n$, and Section~\ref{sec:fisher_explicit} gives both in
closed form, so $\mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}$ can be computed from the data
alone.

\textbf{2. Only local regularity is used.}
The proof uses about $\hat{\theta}_n$ only that it is locally regular at
$\theta_0$ with limit law $N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}}$.
Localizability is part of Definition~\ref{def:regular}, and consistency
in probability follows, since local regularity forces
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ and hence
$\hat{\bm{u}}_n \to \bm{0}$ in probability.
Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence} therefore holds verbatim for any locally
regular sequence with that limit law, $\hat{\theta}_n$ being replaced by
it in \eqref{eq:confidence_set} and $\mathcal{A}_n$ by the corresponding
localization event; by \eqref{eq:convolution} these are exactly the
locally regular sequences that attain the convolution bound.

\textbf{3. What ``locally uniform'' does and does not mean.}
Statement \eqref{eq:coverage} is made along each fixed sequence of local
alternatives, which is the exact strength of Definition~\ref{def:regular}
and is already enough to rule out the failure exhibited in
Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges}.
Indeed, at $\theta_0 = \theta_*$ the Hodges-type estimator is pinned to
$\theta_*$ with $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$-probability tending to
one, so that on that event the analogous region is the
\textit{deterministic} set
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_*}\bracket{\curly{\bm{v} :
n\,G_{\theta_*}(\bm{v},\bm{v}) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}}}$, which by
the injectivity of $\text{Exp}_{\theta_*}$ contains
$\theta_{n,\bm{h}}
= \text{Exp}_{\theta_*}\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}$ precisely when
$G(\bm{h},\bm{h}) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}$, a condition free of $n$.
Its coverage of $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ therefore tends to $1$ when
$G(\bm{h},\bm{h}) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}$ and to $0$ otherwise, and
equals $1-\alpha$ in the limit for no $\bm{h}$ at all, $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$
included.
The genuinely uniform statement,
$\sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant c}
\abs{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}}-(1-\alpha)}\to 0$,
would require the expansion \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} along arbitrary
bounded sequences $\bm{h}_n$ rather than along fixed $\bm{h}$, which
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} as stated does not provide; we do not need it
anywhere.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{The fitted covariance matrix}

The second consequence records what all of this says about the object the
model is really about, namely the fitted covariance matrix
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n$ of \eqref{eq:Sigma_hat_def}, which, unlike
$\hat{\theta}_n$ itself, is an honest element of the fixed vector space
$\text{Sym}(p)$.
It is the instance $\varphi = \bm{\Sigma}$ of item 4 above, and the linear
map $\mathcal{D}_0$ of \eqref{eq:D_0_def} plays the role of
$\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\theta_0}$: it is the differential
$\mathrm{d}\bm{\Sigma}_{\theta_0}$ of the map $\bm{\Sigma}$ of
\eqref{eq:Sigma_Phi_def}, read through the exponential chart.

\begin{corollary}[Covariance level central limit theorem]\label{thm:Sigma_CLT}
Let $1 \leqslant q < p$ and $\theta_0 \in M$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ the fitted covariance matrix is
  asymptotically linear in $\text{Sym}(p)$ with influence function
  $\mathcal{D}_0 \circ \text{IF}$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_linear}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}
    = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n
    \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{\text{IF}(\bm{X}_i)} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
  \end{equation}
  and consequently
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_CLT}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}
    \rightsquigarrow \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z}),
    \qquad \bm{Z} \sim N\bracket{\bm{0},\ G^{-1}}
    \text{ on } T_{\theta_0}M .
  \end{equation}
  \item The map $\mathcal{D}_0$ is injective, and, in the notation of
  Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}, its image is
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:D_0_image}
    \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{T_{\theta_0}M}
    = \curly{\bm{J}_0
    \begin{pmatrix} \bm{M}_{11} & \bm{M}_{21}^T \\
    \bm{M}_{21} & \beta\bm{I}_{p-q}\end{pmatrix}\bm{J}_0^T
    \ : \ \bm{M}_{11} \in \text{Sym}(q),\
    \bm{M}_{21} \in \mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q},\ \beta \in \mathbb{R}},
  \end{equation}
  where $\bm{J}_0 := \squared{\bm{U}_0 \ \ \bm{U}_0^{\perp}} \in O(p)$ as
  in \eqref{eq:J_0_def} below.
  Hence the limit in \eqref{eq:Sigma_CLT} is a centred Gaussian law
  carried by a $d$-dimensional subspace of $\text{Sym}(p)$, namely the
  tangent space at $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ to the immersed submanifold
  $\bm{\Sigma}(M)$ of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical}; it is
  degenerate in $\text{Sym}(p)$ exactly when $p-q \geqslant 2$, the
  codimension being
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_codim}
    \frac{p(p+1)}{2} - d
    = \frac{(p-q)(p-q+1)}{2} - 1
    = \frac{(p-q+2)(p-q-1)}{2} .
  \end{equation}
  \item The convergence \eqref{eq:Sigma_CLT} is locally regular: for every
  $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_regular}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n
    - \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}
    \rightsquigarrow \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z})
    \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}},
  \end{equation}
  with the same limit law, free of $\bm{h}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.}
On $\mathcal{A}_n$ we have
$\hat{\theta}_n = \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$, so
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} at $\bm{u} = \hat{\bm{u}}_n$ is the exact
identity

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_hat_exact}
  \hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0
  = \mathcal{D}_0(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)
  + \bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n ,
\end{equation}
with no remainder, because $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ is affine in the
horizontal lift.
Since $\iota$ is a linear isometry,
$\|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T\|_F
\leqslant \|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\|\,\|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\|_F
\leqslant \|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\|_F^2
\leqslant \abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^2$, and
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$ by
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}; hence
$\sqrt{n}\,\|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T\|_F
= O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}} = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.
Multiplying \eqref{eq:Sigma_hat_exact} by $\sqrt{n}$ therefore gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_delta}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}
  = \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n ,
\end{equation}
and the restriction to $\mathcal{A}_n$ is harmless: the two sides of
\eqref{eq:Sigma_delta} differ only on $\mathcal{A}_n^c$, whose probability
tends to zero by Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}, so their
difference is $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ and they have the same weak limits.
Now \eqref{eq:Sigma_linear} follows from \eqref{eq:asc_linearity} and the
linearity of $\mathcal{D}_0$, and \eqref{eq:Sigma_CLT} from
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}, Slutsky's lemma and the continuous
mapping theorem applied to the linear, hence continuous, map
$\mathcal{D}_0$.

\textbf{2.}
If $\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) = \bm{0}$ then $G(\bm{h},\bm{h}) = 0$ by
\eqref{eq:G_def}, whence $\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}; so
$\mathcal{D}_0$ is injective and its image has dimension $d$.
That image is the one described in \eqref{eq:D_0_image} by
\eqref{eq:Dh_blocks} of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form} below, together with
part 3 of that lemma and Lemma~\ref{thm:block_coords}: as $\bm{h}$ ranges
over $T_{\theta_0}M$, the triple
$\bracket{2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q,\ \bm{C}\bm{D}_0,\ b_{\bm{h}}}$
ranges over all of
$\text{Sym}(q)\times\mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q}\times\mathbb{R}$, the
matrix $\bm{D}_0$ being invertible.
The image is that of $\mathrm{d}\bm{\Sigma}_{\theta_0}$ because
$\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) = \frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}
\bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(t\bm{h})}$ by
\eqref{eq:Sigma_directional} and $t \mapsto \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(t\bm{h})$
has velocity $\bm{h}$ at $t=0$.
Reading \eqref{eq:D_0_image} off, the only constraint that
$\mathcal{D}_0(T_{\theta_0}M)$ imposes on a symmetric matrix is that its
trailing $(p-q)\times(p-q)$ block, in the basis $\bm{J}_0$, be a multiple
of the identity; the codimension is therefore
$\dim \text{Sym}(p-q)-1 = \frac{(p-q)(p-q+1)}{2}-1$.
Writing $m := p-q$, this is
$\frac{m^2+m-2}{2} = \frac{(m+2)(m-1)}{2}$, and it vanishes if and only if
$m = 1$, since $m+2 \geqslant 3$.
The same number is $\frac{p(p+1)}{2}-d$: by \eqref{eq:d_def},

\begin{equation*}
  \frac{p(p+1)}{2} - d
  = \frac{p(p+1)}{2} - pq - 1 + \frac{q(q-1)}{2}
  = \frac{p^2 - 2pq + q^2 + p - q - 2}{2}
  = \frac{m^2+m-2}{2} .
\end{equation*}

\textbf{3.}
Fix $\bm{h}$ and work under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, which is
contiguous to $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ by Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}; in
particular $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$ and the
$O_{\mathbb{P}}$ statements above persist, as in the proof of
Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence}.
Applying \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} at $\bm{u} = \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}$
gives the second exact identity

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} - \bm{\Sigma}_0
  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h})
  + \frac{1}{n}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T ,
\end{equation*}
so subtracting it from \eqref{eq:Sigma_hat_exact} and multiplying by
$\sqrt{n}$,

\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n
  - \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}
  = \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}
  + \sqrt{n}\,\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T
  - \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n .
\end{equation*}
The last term is deterministic and $O\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$.
By \eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion},
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n-\bm{h} \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$; in particular
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ there, so the middle term is
again $O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}}$ by the bound of part 1.
The continuous mapping theorem applied to $\mathcal{D}_0$ now gives
\eqref{eq:Sigma_regular}, the limit being the image of
$N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$, that is $\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z})$, and free of
$\bm{h}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is used where]\label{rmk:what_is_added}
Part 1 of Corollary~\ref{thm:Sigma_CLT} uses nothing from
Section~\ref{sec:regularity} beyond the localization of
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}, and even there any normal
neighbourhood of $\theta_0$ would serve in place of $\mathcal{V}$.
Its proof invokes Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}, the exact
expansion \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} and the continuous mapping theorem,
and no more; neither the parallel transport of
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport}, nor the transport comparison of
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison}, nor the contiguity of
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}, nor the local regularity of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} appears anywhere in it.
It could have been stated at the end of Section~\ref{sec:MLE}; it is
placed here only because part 3 belongs here, and because it is the
covariance level reading of the whole chapter.

The reason no transport is needed is the converse of the phenomenon that
made Section~\ref{sec:regularity} necessary in the first place.
The intrinsic error
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\hat{\theta}_n)$ of
Definition~\ref{def:regular} lives in $T_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}M$, a vector
space that \textit{moves with $n$}, which is exactly why
$\Gamma_{\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}}$ has to be inserted before any limit can be
taken.
The covariance level error
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}(\theta)$, by contrast, is a difference
of two elements of the single fixed vector space $\text{Sym}(p)$, for
every $\theta$ at once; there is nothing to transport, the delta method
applies verbatim, and curvature is invisible because
$\bm{\Sigma}(M) \subseteq \text{Sym}(p)$ is being viewed extrinsically.
That the delta method is here an identity rather than an approximation is
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic}: the quadratic remainder is written out and
cancelled, so \eqref{eq:Sigma_regular} is a sharper form of the general
\eqref{eq:delta_regular}.

What part 3 costs is correspondingly small: given
\eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion}, which is the substance of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} and does use Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} and
Le Cam's third lemma, the passage to \eqref{eq:Sigma_regular} is three
lines of exact algebra.
One should resist reading more into part 3 than it says: it asserts that
the \textit{maximum likelihood} fitted covariance is locally regular as an
estimator of $\bm{\Sigma}(\theta)$, and nothing about other estimators.

This is the place to close the account opened at the start of the
section.
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} is a statement about \textit{other
parameters} than $\theta_0$, and none of the geometry it rests on ---
Lemma~\ref{thm:lyapunov_lemma},
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} and
Lemmas~\ref{thm:uniform_chart} and \ref{thm:transport_comparison}, which
are results about $(M,g)$ in their own right, independent of any
statistics --- was needed by
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics}, which lives entirely inside the
single vector space $T_{\theta_0}M$.
They are what the two-base-point comparison costs, and
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} is the one probabilistic ingredient added
alongside them.
In the terminology of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026},
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} verifies their Definition III.5 for the
probabilistic principal component analysis model.
We have proved it through the chart reduction of
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} and the classical Euclidean results of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, rather than by citing the manifold version,
because Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} makes that reduction exact,
and because of the localization of the curvature recorded in
Remark~\ref{rmk:transport_scale}.
\end{remark}

\section{The Fisher Information Operator in Closed Form}\label{sec:fisher_explicit}

Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} identifies the limiting covariance
of the maximum likelihood estimator as $G^{-1}_{\theta_0}$, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} shows that this limit is not an accident of
the point $\theta_0$; but nothing so far says what $G^{-1}_{\theta_0}$
\textit{is}.
This section computes it.
The only inputs are \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional}, \eqref{eq:score} and
\eqref{eq:G_def} of Section~\ref{sec:PPCA_properties}, the spectral
decomposition \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} of
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} and the horizontal lift
\eqref{eq:lift_def}; no asymptotics are used until
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT_coordinates}, which merely reads
Proposition~\ref{thm:MLE_asymptotics} in the coordinates constructed here.

Two features of the answer deserve to be announced.
First, the whole calculation is a $2 \times 2$ block computation in the
singular basis of $\bm{W}_0$, and it produces a set of coordinates in
which $G_{\theta_0}$ is block diagonal with blocks of size $1$, $1$ and
$q+1$; the only genuinely coupled block is the one that mixes the $q$
leading directions with the noise variance, and it is inverted by a
one-line Schur complement.
Second, no assumption whatsoever is made on the multiplicities of the
singular values of $\bm{W}_0$: the formulas below are valid when
$\sigma_1(\bm{W}_0) = \cdots = \sigma_q(\bm{W}_0)$, a case in which
individual eigenvectors of $\bm{S}_n$ are not asymptotically normal at
all.
This is the quantitative form of the third comment of
Remark~\ref{rmk:MLE_CLT_comments}.

\subsection*{Block coordinates at the true parameter}

Everything rests on the spectral decomposition of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$, which
the first chapter computes eigenvalue by eigenvalue in
\eqref{eq:lambda_j_Sigma_0} but does not assemble into a basis.
We assemble it now.

\begin{lemma}[Spectral decomposition of the true covariance]\label{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}
Fix a singular value decomposition
$\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$, in which
$\bm{U}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ has orthonormal columns,
$\bm{V}_0 \in O(q)$, and
$\bm{D}_0 = \text{diag}\bracket{\sigma_1(\bm{W}_0),\ldots,\sigma_q(\bm{W}_0)}$
has strictly positive diagonal because $\rank(\bm{W}_0) = q$.
Put $\bm{P}_0 := \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$, the orthogonal projection onto the
column space of $\bm{W}_0$.
Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization}
  \bm{\Sigma}_0
  = \bm{U}_0\bracket{\bm{D}_0^2+\sigma_0^2\bm{I}_q}\bm{U}_0^T
  + \sigma_0^2\bracket{\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0} ,
\end{equation}
the two summands acting on orthogonal complementary subspaces; this is the
spectral decomposition realizing the eigenvalues
\eqref{eq:lambda_j_Sigma_0}, and the gap separating the signal
eigenvalues from the noise eigenvalue is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:eigengap}
  \lambda_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)
  = \sigma_q(\bm{W}_0)^2 > 0 .
\end{equation}
Passing to another representative $(\bm{W}_0\bm{Q},\sigma_0^2)$ of
$\theta_0$, with $\bm{Q} \in O(q)$, leaves $\bm{U}_0$, $\bm{D}_0$ and
$\bm{P}_0$ unchanged and replaces $\bm{V}_0$ by $\bm{Q}^T\bm{V}_0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We have $\bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0^2\bm{U}_0^T$ and
$\bm{I}_p = \bm{P}_0+\bracket{\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0}$, whence
\eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization}; the ranges of $\bm{P}_0$ and
$\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0$ are orthogonal complements.
Equation \eqref{eq:eigengap} follows from \eqref{eq:lambda_j_Sigma_0}
because $\sigma_q(\bm{W}_0) = \sigma_{\min}(\bm{W}_0) > 0$.
The last sentence is immediate from
$\bm{W}_0\bm{Q} = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bracket{\bm{Q}^T\bm{V}_0}^T$.
\end{proof}

We fix the singular value decomposition
$\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$ of
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} once and for all, and we complete
$\bm{U}_0$ to an orthogonal matrix: we let
$\bm{U}_0^{\perp} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times (p-q)}$ have orthonormal
columns spanning the orthogonal complement of the column space of
$\bm{W}_0$, so that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:J_0_def}
  \bm{J}_0 := \squared{\bm{U}_0 \ \ \bm{U}_0^{\perp}} \in O(p),
  \qquad
  \bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bracket{\bm{U}_0^{\perp}}^T .
\end{equation}
We abbreviate the signal eigenvalues of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ supplied by
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} as

\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau_def}
  \tau_j := \lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2 + \sigma_0^2
  > \sigma_0^2 \qquad (j = 1,\ldots,q),
  \qquad
  \bm{T}_0 := \bm{D}_0^2 + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_q
  = \text{diag}\bracket{\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_q} .
\end{equation}
The letters $\bm{B}$, $\bm{C}$, $\bm{\Psi}$, $\bm{y}$, $\bm{z}$,
$\bm{J}_0$, $\bm{T}_0$, $\tau_j$, $\kappa$, $\bm{K}$ and $\mathcal{G}$ are
local to this section.

\begin{lemma}[Block form of the differential]\label{thm:block_form}
Let $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ have horizontal lift
$\iota(\bm{h}) = \bracket{\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b_{\bm{h}}}$ as in
\eqref{eq:iota_zero}, and set

\begin{equation}\label{eq:B_C_Psi_def}
  \bm{B} := \bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{V}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{q\times q},
  \qquad
  \bm{C} := \bracket{\bm{U}_0^{\perp}}^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{V}_0
  \in \mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q},
  \qquad
  \bm{\Psi} := \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 + \bm{D}_0\bm{B}^T}
  \in \text{Sym}(q).
\end{equation}
Then:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix} \bm{T}_0 & \bm{0}\\
  \bm{0} & \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_{p-q}\end{pmatrix}$
  and
  $\bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix} \bm{T}_0^{-1} & \bm{0}\\
  \bm{0} & \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{I}_{p-q}\end{pmatrix}$.
  \item $\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}
  = \bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{V}_0^T + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{V}_0^T$
  and
  $\abs{\bm{h}}^2 = \|\bm{B}\|_F^2 + \|\bm{C}\|_F^2 + b_{\bm{h}}^2$.
  \item $\bm{D}_0\bm{B} \in \text{Sym}(q)$; conversely every triple
  $(\bm{B},\bm{C},b)$ with $\bm{D}_0\bm{B}$ symmetric arises from exactly
  one $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$.
  \item In the basis \eqref{eq:J_0_def},
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Dh_blocks}
    \bm{J}_0^T\, \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h})\, \bm{J}_0
    = \begin{pmatrix}
      2\bm{\Psi} + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q & \bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T \\
      \bm{C}\bm{D}_0 & b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_{p-q}
    \end{pmatrix},
  \end{equation}
  with $\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) = D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)$ as in
  \eqref{eq:D_0_def}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.} Substitute $\bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$ and
$\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0^{\perp}(\bm{U}_0^{\perp})^T$ into
\eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} and use
$\bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_0 = \bm{I}_q$,
$(\bm{U}_0^{\perp})^T\bm{U}_0^{\perp} = \bm{I}_{p-q}$ and
$\bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_0^{\perp} = \bm{0}$.

\textbf{2.} Since $\bm{J}_0 \in O(p)$ and $\bm{V}_0 \in O(q)$, the matrix
$\bm{J}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{V}_0$ has $\bm{B}$ and $\bm{C}$ stacked as
its blocks, which is the first identity; the Frobenius norm is invariant
under multiplication by orthogonal matrices on either side, so
$\|\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\|_F^2 = \|\bm{B}\|_F^2+\|\bm{C}\|_F^2$, and the second
identity is \eqref{eq:lift_properties}.

\textbf{3.} By part 3 of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_vertical} the
pair $(\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b_{\bm{h}})$ is horizontal at
$(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$ if and only if
$\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T\bm{W}_0 = \bm{W}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}$.
Using part 2 and $\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$,

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{W}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}
  = \bm{V}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T
  \bracket{\bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{V}_0^T + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{V}_0^T}
  = \bm{V}_0\bracket{\bm{D}_0\bm{B}}\bm{V}_0^T,
\end{equation*}
which is symmetric if and only if $\bm{D}_0\bm{B}$ is.
Conversely, given such a triple, part 2 defines $\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}$, the
displayed identity makes $(\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b)$ horizontal, and
$\bm{h} := \mathrm{d}\pi_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}\bracket{(\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b)}$
is the unique preimage because $\iota$ is a bijection onto
$H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$.

\textbf{4.} By \eqref{eq:D_0_def},
$\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) = \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T
+ \bm{W}_0\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p$, and by part 2,

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T
  = \bracket{\bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{V}_0^T + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{V}_0^T}
  \bm{V}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T
  = \bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T
  + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T,
\end{equation*}
so that
$\bm{J}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T\bm{J}_0
= \begin{pmatrix} \bm{B}\bm{D}_0 & \bm{0}\\
\bm{C}\bm{D}_0 & \bm{0}\end{pmatrix}$.
Adding the transpose of this matrix and $b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p$, and using
$\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 + \bracket{\bm{B}\bm{D}_0}^T = 2\bm{\Psi}$, gives
\eqref{eq:Dh_blocks}.
\end{proof}

The three blocks have direct meanings, and it is worth recording them
before any computation.
By \eqref{eq:Dh_blocks} the matrix $2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q$ is the
first order change of $\bm{\Sigma}$ inside the signal block, the matrix
$\bm{C}\bm{D}_0$ is its change in the signal--noise block, and
$b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_{p-q}$ is its change in the noise block --- where the
model forces the change to be a \textit{multiple of the identity}, which
is the whole source of the efficiency gain computed below, and which is
the only constraint, as \eqref{eq:D_0_image} recorded.
Note also that $\bm{\Psi}$, not $\bm{B}$, is the natural unknown: the map
$\bm{B} \mapsto \bm{\Psi}$ is exactly the operation that quotients out the
$\frac{q(q-1)}{2}$ directions along which $\bm{\Sigma}$ does not move.

\begin{lemma}[Linear coordinates on $T_{\theta_0}M$]\label{thm:block_coords}
The map

\begin{equation}\label{eq:kappa_def}
  \kappa : T_{\theta_0}M \longrightarrow
  \text{Sym}(q) \times \mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q}\times\mathbb{R},
  \qquad
  \kappa(\bm{h}) := \bracket{\bm{\Psi},\ \bm{C},\ b_{\bm{h}}}
\end{equation}
of \eqref{eq:B_C_Psi_def} is a linear isomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Linearity is clear: $\iota$ is linear by \eqref{eq:lift_def}, and
$\bm{U}_{\bm{h}} \mapsto (\bm{B},\bm{C}) \mapsto \bm{\Psi}$ is a
composition of linear maps.

For injectivity, suppose $\kappa(\bm{h}) = \bm{0}$.
Then $b_{\bm{h}} = 0$ and $\bm{C} = \bm{0}$, while $\bm{\Psi} = \bm{0}$
reads $\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 = -\bm{D}_0\bm{B}^T$.
Since $\rank(\bm{W}_0) = q$, all $\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)$ are strictly
positive and $\bm{D}_0$ is invertible, so part 3 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form}, that is $\bm{D}_0\bm{B} = \bm{B}^T\bm{D}_0$,
gives $\bm{B}^T = \bm{D}_0\bm{B}\bm{D}_0^{-1}$.
Substituting this into $\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 = -\bm{D}_0\bm{B}^T$ yields
$\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 = -\bm{D}_0^2\bm{B}\bm{D}_0^{-1}$, and multiplying on the
right by $\bm{D}_0$,

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{B}\bm{D}_0^2 + \bm{D}_0^2\bm{B} = \bm{0}.
\end{equation*}
Both $\bm{D}_0^2$ factors are diagonal, so the $(i,j)$ entry of the left
hand side is
$\bracket{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2 + \sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2}\bm{B}_{ij}$, and
the bracket is strictly positive; hence $\bm{B} = \bm{0}$.
By part 2 of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form} this forces
$\bm{U}_{\bm{h}} = \bm{0}$, so $\iota(\bm{h}) = (\bm{0},0)$ and
$\bm{h} = \bm{0}$.

For surjectivity it suffices to compare dimensions, $\kappa$ being
injective and linear:

\begin{equation*}
  \dim\bracket{\text{Sym}(q)\times\mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q}\times\mathbb{R}}
  = \frac{q(q+1)}{2} + (p-q)q + 1
  = pq + 1 - \frac{q(q-1)}{2}
  = d
\end{equation*}
by \eqref{eq:d_def}.
\end{proof}

Lemma~\ref{thm:block_coords} lets us treat $(\bm{\Psi},\bm{C},b)$ as
coordinates on $T_{\theta_0}M$.
We use throughout the \textit{scalar} coordinates

\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar_coords}
  \bracket{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}_{1\leqslant i \leqslant j \leqslant q},
  \qquad
  \bracket{\bm{C}_{ki}}_{1\leqslant k \leqslant p-q,\ 1\leqslant i \leqslant q},
  \qquad
  b ,
\end{equation}
which identify the target of $\kappa$ with $\mathbb{R}^d$, and we let
$\mathcal{G}$ denote the $d \times d$ symmetric matrix representing the
Fisher form in them, that is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gcal_def}
  G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2)
  = \kappa(\bm{h}_1)^T\,\mathcal{G}\,\kappa(\bm{h}_2)
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2 \in T_{\theta_0}M .
\end{equation}
Note that $\kappa$ is \textit{not} a $g_{\theta_0}$-isometry, so
$\mathcal{G}$ is not the matrix $\bm{G}$ of \eqref{eq:Delta_n_def}; the
relation between the two is recorded in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT_coordinates} and is the only place where it is
needed.

\subsection*{The Fisher form and its inverse}

\begin{proposition}[Explicit Fisher form]\label{thm:G_explicit}
For every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$, in the notation
\eqref{eq:B_C_Psi_def},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_blocks}
  G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h},\bm{h})
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}
  \bracket{2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q}}^2}
  + \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}\tr\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0^2\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}
  + \frac{(p-q)\,b_{\bm{h}}^2}{2\sigma_0^4},
\end{equation}
and, in the scalar coordinates \eqref{eq:scalar_coords},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_scalar}
  G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h},\bm{h})
  = \underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^q
  \frac{\bracket{2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}+b_{\bm{h}}}^2}{2\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{(p-q)b_{\bm{h}}^2}{2\sigma_0^4}}_{\text{coupled block}}
  \ + \underbrace{\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant q}
  \frac{4\bm{\Psi}_{ij}^2}{\tau_i\tau_j}}_{\bm{\Psi}\text{ off-diagonal}}
  \ + \underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^q\sum_{k=1}^{p-q}
  \bracket{\frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}}
  \bm{C}_{ki}^2}_{\bm{C}\text{-block}} .
\end{equation}
In particular $\mathcal{G}$ is block diagonal with respect to the
partition of \eqref{eq:scalar_coords} into the three groups
$\curly{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}_{i<j}$, $\curly{\bm{C}_{ki}}$ and
$\bracket{\bm{\Psi}_{11},\ldots,\bm{\Psi}_{qq},b}$, with

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gcal_blocks}
  \mathcal{G}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}\bm{\Psi}_{ij}} = \frac{4}{\tau_i\tau_j}
  \quad (i<j),
  \qquad
  \mathcal{G}_{\bm{C}_{ki}\bm{C}_{ki}} = \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}
  = \frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i},
  \qquad
  \mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}
  = \begin{pmatrix}
  \bm{\Pi} & \bm{\nu} \\
  \bm{\nu}^T & \eta
  \end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
the last being the coupled block in the ordering
$\bracket{\bm{\Psi}_{11},\ldots,\bm{\Psi}_{qq},b}$, where

\begin{equation}\label{eq:v_gamma_def}
  \bm{\Pi} := \text{diag}\bracket{2\tau_1^{-2},\ldots,2\tau_q^{-2}},
  \qquad
  \bm{\nu} := \bracket{\tau_1^{-2},\ldots,\tau_q^{-2}}^T \in \mathbb{R}^q,
  \qquad
  \eta := \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{p-q}{2\sigma_0^4} .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $\bm{M} := 2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q$ and $b := b_{\bm{h}}$.
Conjugating by $\bm{J}_0$ does not change traces, so by \eqref{eq:G_def}
with $\bm{h}_1 = \bm{h}_2 = \bm{h}$ it suffices to compute the trace of
the square of
$\bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h})\bm{J}_0$.
Multiplying the two matrices of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_inv_Dh}
  \bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h})\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix}
    \bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M} & \bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T \\
    \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0 & \sigma_0^{-2}b\,\bm{I}_{p-q}
  \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
The two diagonal blocks of the square of \eqref{eq:Sigma_inv_Dh} are

\begin{equation*}
  \bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M}}^2
  + \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T\bm{C}\bm{D}_0
  \qquad \text{and} \qquad
  \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T
  + \sigma_0^{-4}b^2\bm{I}_{p-q},
\end{equation*}
so, using the cyclic property of the trace and the fact that the diagonal
matrices $\bm{T}_0$ and $\bm{D}_0$ commute,

\begin{equation*}
  \tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h})}^2}
  = \tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M}}^2}
  + \frac{2}{\sigma_0^2}\tr\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0^2\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}
  + \frac{(p-q)b^2}{\sigma_0^4} .
\end{equation*}
Halving gives \eqref{eq:G_blocks}.

For \eqref{eq:G_scalar}, both $\bm{T}_0^{-1}$ and $\bm{D}_0^2$ are
diagonal, so

\begin{equation*}
  \tr\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0^2\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}
  = \sum_{i=1}^q \frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\tau_i}
  \bracket{\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}_{ii}
  = \sum_{i=1}^q \frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\tau_i}
  \sum_{k=1}^{p-q}\bm{C}_{ki}^2,
\end{equation*}
and $\frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}
= \frac{\tau_i-\sigma_0^2}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}
= \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}$ by \eqref{eq:tau_def}.
For the first term, $\bm{M}$ is symmetric, so

\begin{equation*}
  \tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M}}^2}
  = \sum_{i,j=1}^q\frac{\bm{M}_{ij}\bm{M}_{ji}}{\tau_i\tau_j}
  = \sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bm{M}_{ii}^2}{\tau_i^2}
  + 2\sum_{i<j}\frac{\bm{M}_{ij}^2}{\tau_i\tau_j},
\end{equation*}
and $\bm{M}_{ii} = 2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}+b$, $\bm{M}_{ij} = 2\bm{\Psi}_{ij}$ for
$i \neq j$; halving gives the first two groups of terms in
\eqref{eq:G_scalar}.

Finally, \eqref{eq:G_scalar} is a sum of three quadratic forms in
\textit{disjoint} sets of the scalar coordinates \eqref{eq:scalar_coords},
which is exactly the assertion of block diagonality; reading off the
coefficients gives the first two entries of \eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}.
For the third, expanding the coupled part of \eqref{eq:G_scalar},

\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bracket{2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}+b}^2}{2\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{(p-q)b^2}{2\sigma_0^4}
  = \sum_{i=1}^q \frac{2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}^2}{\tau_i^2}
  + 2b\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}{\tau_i^2}
  + b^2\bracket{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{p-q}{2\sigma_0^4}},
\end{equation*}
whose matrix in the ordering
$\bracket{\bm{\Psi}_{11},\ldots,\bm{\Psi}_{qq},b}$ is
$\mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}$ of \eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}: the coefficient of
$\bm{\Psi}_{ii}^2$ is $2\tau_i^{-2} = \bm{\Pi}_{ii}$, that of
$b\,\bm{\Psi}_{ii}$ is $2\tau_i^{-2} = 2\bm{\nu}_i$, and that of $b^2$ is
$\eta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Explicit inverse]\label{thm:G_inverse_explicit}
The matrix $\mathcal{G}$ of \eqref{eq:Gcal_def} is invertible, and
$\mathcal{G}^{-1}$ is block diagonal with respect to the same partition,
with

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ginv_diagonal}
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}
  = \frac{\tau_i\tau_j}{4} \quad (i<j),
  \qquad
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{C}_{ki}\bm{C}_{ki}}
  = \frac{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}
  = \bracket{\frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}}^{-1},
\end{equation}
these two groups being diagonal, and, on the coupled block,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ginv_coupled}
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{bb} = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q},
  \qquad
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}b} = -\frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q},
  \qquad
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}\bm{\Psi}_{jj}}
  = \delta_{ij}\frac{\tau_i^2}{2} + \frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)} .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\mathcal{G}$ is positive definite because $G_{\theta_0}$ is, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, and $\kappa$ is a bijection; so it is invertible,
and the inverse of a block diagonal matrix is block diagonal with the
blocks inverted.
The first two groups are diagonal by \eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}, and their
inverses are the reciprocals, which is \eqref{eq:Ginv_diagonal}.

For the coupled block, $\bm{\Pi}^{-1}
= \text{diag}\bracket{\frac{\tau_1^2}{2},\ldots,\frac{\tau_q^2}{2}}$ and

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Pinv_v}
  \bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu}
  = \bracket{\frac{\tau_i^2}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}}_{i=1}^q
  = \frac{1}{2}\bm{1}_q ,
\end{equation}
so the Schur complement of $\bm{\Pi}$ in $\mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}$ is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:schur}
  \zeta := \eta - \bm{\nu}^T\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu}
  = \eta - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}
  = \frac{p-q}{2\sigma_0^4}
\end{equation}
by \eqref{eq:v_gamma_def}; it is strictly positive because $q<p$, which is
where the standing assumption $q<p$ enters the inversion.
The standard block inversion formula

\begin{equation*}
  \begin{pmatrix}\bm{\Pi} & \bm{\nu}\\
  \bm{\nu}^T & \eta\end{pmatrix}^{-1}
  = \begin{pmatrix}
  \bm{\Pi}^{-1}
  + \zeta^{-1}\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu}\bm{\nu}^T\bm{\Pi}^{-1}
  & -\zeta^{-1}\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu} \\
  -\zeta^{-1}\bm{\nu}^T\bm{\Pi}^{-1} & \zeta^{-1}
  \end{pmatrix}
\end{equation*}
together with \eqref{eq:Pinv_v} and \eqref{eq:schur} gives
$\zeta^{-1} = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$, then
$-\zeta^{-1}\bracket{\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu}}_i
= -\frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\cdot\frac{1}{2}
= -\frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$, and finally

\begin{equation*}
  \bracket{\bm{\Pi}^{-1}}_{ij}
  + \zeta^{-1}\bracket{\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu}}_i
  \bracket{\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\bm{\nu}}_j
  = \delta_{ij}\frac{\tau_i^2}{2}
